152. 领读Kimi K3技术报告：从架构创新聊起，注意力美学、多教师蒸馏和开源MoE

Zhang Xiaojun Podcast(41.5K)·124:20·2.8K views·2026-08-26
Yutao SunPhD Candidate, Tsinghua University
TL;DRKimi K3 represents a major milestone as a 2.8T parameter fully open-source MoE model that successfully integrates hybrid attention, advanced routing, and efficient infrastructure.
Guest expert Sun Yutao, a Tsinghua University PhD candidate, breaks down the core architectural...

TL;DR

Kimi K3 represents a major milestone as a 2.8T parameter fully open-source MoE model that successfully integrates hybrid attention, advanced routing, and efficient infrastructure. Guest expert Sun Yutao, a Tsinghua University PhD candidate, breaks down the core architectural innovations, training recipes, and engineering CoDesign that enable K3's scale and 1-million-token context length.

Model Architecture & Innovations

  • Hybrid Attention Design:
    • Replaces pure attention or pure linear attention with a hybrid structure (approximately 3:1 ratio of linear to full attention in K3).
    • Proves that mixing attention types does not sacrifice context performance and allows lossless or superior long-context behavior while saving compute.
  • Kimi Linear & Fine-Grained Decay:
    • Evolves from RetNet, Mamba-2, and Gated DeltaNet by moving from a scalar decay to a channel-wise ($\text{channel-wise}$) decay.
    • Implements a lower-bound decay ($\text{lower bound decay}$) to keep numerical values within the $\text{BF16}$ dynamic range inside a 16-token tile window, enabling efficient TensorCore utilization.
  • Gated MLA (Multi-head Latent Attention):
    • Combines DeepSeek-style MLA with gated attention mechanisms to significantly improve pretraining stability.
    • Solves the training collapse and outlier explosion issues commonly seen in large-scale MoE runs without sacrificing inference efficiency.
  • Attention Residuals & Normalization:
    • Leverages deeper residual connections inspired by HyperConnection to increase the model's residual capacity with near-zero inference overhead.
    • Introduces soft-clipping ($\text{Soft Clip}$) in activation functions (like C2GLU) to strictly bound internal hidden state outliers and stabilize training.

Training, Scaling, & Long-Context Strategies

  • Scaling Efficiency:
    • K3 reaches 2.8T total parameters with ~1.04B active parameters—delivering roughly 2.5x higher scaling efficiency compared to K2.
    • Utilizes Cosine Decay over WSD (Warmup-Stable-Decay) due to easier hyperparameter tuning and more predictable decay ratios.
  • Native 1M Long-Context via NoPE:
    • Replaces RoPE (Rotary Position Embedding) with NoPE (No Position Embedding) in full attention layers within the hybrid setup.
    • Eliminates the need to adjust position embedding parameters when extending context windows, avoiding the long-context degradation caused by raw RoPE.
  • Post-Training & Distillation (OPD):
    • Defers FP8/low-precision quantization to the SFT stage rather than full-scratch low-precision training, ensuring project stability.
    • Employs On-Policy Distillation (OPD / reverse KL) to have student models generate paths while teacher models provide step-by-step corrections.

Infrastructure & Engineering CoDesign

  • MoE Expert Routing & Counter Balancing:
    • Implements Counter Balancing (QB) to replace heuristic loss-free routing with a principled, analytical bias adjustment computed via histogram binning across distributed DP ranks.
  • Distributed Training & Offloading:
    • Uses MoEP (Mixture-of-Experts Parallelism) with a small percentage of redundant experts to dynamically align token counts across GPUs and eliminate execution bubble waste.
    • Leverages aggressive CPU offloading in FP8 for activation states, overlapping communication with Shared Expert compute paths.
  • Multimodal Infrastructure Handling:
    • Manages visual token compression and uneven pipeline parallelism (PP) workloads by shifting vision encoders and idle layers to optimize pipeline bubble fill rates.

Notable Quotes

"纯线性注意力作为纯线性注意力的模型而言，无疑是一个比较失败的一个尝试... 所以当时我们在23年也是注意到了这个现象，所以我们就会尝试从全线性注意力展到一个Hybrid-Attention的架构形式。" — 孙宇涛

>

"大模型可能没有太本质的创新了，后面都是一些改良性的进步... 技术永远是慢慢去提升，然后靠这个公司里的所有人向模型里，包括整个业界的同学一块去推动的。" — 孙宇涛
Hello, 大家好。我是小俊。今天的节目是一期领读博客，领读Kimi K3的技术报告，希望和大家一起领略技术之美。K3是有效扩展到2.8T总参数并全量开源的MOE模型。大家可能注意到，这期技术报告的领读博客距离模型发布已经过去了一段时间。期间，我们试图寻找一位合适的嘉宾，我们希望这个嘉宾他的工作和学术背景非常适合来讲K3，最后找到了孙宇涛。宇涛目前是清华大学计算计系博士候选人，上海创智学院普瑞学者。他从博士开始的研究方向是LLM架构、预训练，架构创新一直都是他的兴趣所在，这正好是K3的亮点之一。那宇涛通过领读K3的论文，也串联讲解了十多篇相关的论文，他的语速非常快，所以前方语速高能预警。期待2026年，我们和AI共同进步。当然了，这块我觉得可能还有一些别的concern。比如，我们发现其实如果用Mixture-of-Experts的话，它其实是会更容易出现Outlier的。近期控制中间的这些或总不是一个比较错的一个现象。当然，如果我说Mixture-of-Experts更容易引来Outlier，那这个缩减力一定回反对。那肯定会说，对于任何优化起来来说，它都一定会出现Outlier，因为你从模型架构上是控制不了的。所以说，如果想严格控制的方法的话，一定就是直接从模型架构下手。这个这在哲学上还有对应的概念，就是Ta-Ky-Su之传。就是你刚开始2017年这个船刚开始行驶的时候，就是Transform。对，但是这个船刚开始是怎么拼起来的呢？它是一个Attention加了一个Residual，那这个过程当中，我们可能会慢漫的把某些部分换了，某些部分换了。其实跟Transformer已经我觉得像So，已经很低很低了。你是否还把这个东西叫做Transformer？我说说我判断，我的判断比较暴论。我的暴论就是大模型可能没有太本质的创新，后面都是一些改动性的，仅仅仅模型。好的，那我们的论文领读博客又来了。这次我邀请了清华大学的PhD candidates，上海创智学院普瑞学者孙宇涛，再有他带领大家一起来学习和阅读最近大家关注度非常高的Kimi K3的论文。但是宇涛会带领大家读一系列的论文。那宇涛，你能不能先给大家做一个自我介绍，并且介绍一下你的研究方向？好的，各位观众朋友大家下午好。然后我是来自清华大学的孙宇涛。我在这个博士期间的主要的研究方向是大模型的架构和预训练。然后这个博士课题呢，我是从23年从那个时候开始，我们主要去研究这个大模型的模型架构的一系列的工作。然后在这个时间点应该是Chat GPT刚刚发布一段时间吧，但是我们的架构研究从其实从Chat GPT发布之前就开始对差不多就开始了。然后我这两三年的主要的一个研究方向就是去主要是去尝试解决，然后大模型在推理这个环节的一些低低效性吧，然后所以说我们主要的一些研究方向都是围着推理高效性来去展开的。然后其实大家也可以注意到说就是在大模型架构这个方面呢，整个业界的研发方向也是逐渐从偏这个性能为导向逐渐变得是以效率为导向。然后在这个方面其实是有一些范式上的变化的，因为可能在Vision时代或者说在ImageNet时代大家还是尝试去达到更精妙的一些架构就是然后去提升模型本身的一个表现。但是在大型号时代，然后我们其实逐渐发现对于模型的性能而言，模型的参数量永远是一个最主要的一个因素。然后在这个在大模型架构本身的一些改性变化呢，其实相对于模型参数本身的提升带来的那个性能提升是相当微小的。但是其实不同的架构在这个模型的推理方面然后性能差异是巨大的，而且这个也是主要决定了模型最后的一个部署的价格，然后其实这两年大家主要在这个方向去努力。然后对于我而言的话，其实我们是在23年开始然后探索这个线性主力，这样的一的一个方向的一个工作。然后我们这个Project-based的工作刚开始主要是解决了两个问题。我觉得从现在阶段这个阶段这主要解决了两个问题，第一个问题就是线性主力怎么能尽量的去在一些比较general的场景下能逼近这个参数力这样的一种性能。因为Linear Kernel从刚开始提出主要是为了去用一个比较Linear Kernel然后去一个Kernelized的方式去拟合，然后2次复杂的度的全主力这样的一种Pattern的，所以所以在这个之前那个时代其实对于这个Linear Attention而言，它的未知信息它其实是不够sensible的。然后其实当在那个时代这个Linear Attention和这个和RNN只是在形式上比较相似，但是从这个建模的特性上来讲呢，RNN其实还是比较强调这个一具体一切Local-T的一些表示，所以说在那个时代我们在Rai-cut上是比较早的引入了一个衰减的一个机制，这样的话可以让这个线性主力在一个有限的这个状态空间里，然后尽量的去建模一些对最后的结果贡献过大的的一些信息，而对然后这个其实刚开始的一个Motivation。除了这个模型性能以外呢，我们当时也是主要比较现在提出了这种Chang-current，有长课外数Re-current这样的一种线性注意力的一种Kernel-ize，就是Kernel的这个计算形式。因为对于这个线性主力来说呢，如果把这个整个的的状态都展开然后在这个Token这个维度去做这个去去做递归的计算的话，它其实从从效率上并不是最优的。但是如果用仿照注意力这种这个计算方式去建模呢，它又享受不到这个整体计算复杂度带来的优越。所以说我们当时就是在这种Parallel-presentation，从一些并行的就是像全主力这样的一种计算形式和这个全递归的这个计算形式之间找到了一个Trade-off，也就是这个块递归的这样的一种计算形式。然后这个Chang-current-Recurrent呢，我们既可以拿到这个整体的计算复杂度上的收益，同时我们能在Kernel-ize层面，然后我们可以进行这样多的去这样Tensor-core，然后把这个Local的一些计算密度都拉满。这个是我们提出来的一个比较有效的计算方式。然后大家其实如果可以关注到说就是之后的一些其实所有的这些线性注意力的改进工作都是基于这样Chang-current-Recurrent的计算形式去去计算的，包括Mamba-2，然后Gate-Delta-Net，包括，然后来到了今天的这个Kimi-Linear-KDA这样的一种计算模式。然后在这个计算大的计算模式之上的话呢，然后又带来了一些新的比较细节上的变化，然后这个可能之后对今天的后面会更详细的来去基地进行讨论。然后当然从现在这个视角，纯线性注意力，作为纯线性注意力的一个模型而言，无疑是一个比较失败的一个尝试，因为无论如何这个比较有限的这样一个Context，它都难以带来就是在这个长上下文中和这个全注意力力比较完全相同的一个性能。这个其实在今天这个时间点我们都知道是不可能的。所以说这个当时我们在23年吧也是注意到了这个现象，所以说我们就会尝试说就是从这个全线性注意力然后展到一个这个Hybrid-Attention的这样一种架构的形式。就是Hybrid-Attention，就是其实大家顾名思义就很简单，包括Kimi K3其实也是这样的嘛，就是用一些线性主力、和一些全主力然后组合起来，然后作为一个模型的一个架构。然后这个本质上听起来像是一个工程上的Trade-off，然后也是一个比较工程的解法，但是虽然如此呢，我们当时就发现一个现象，不过这个也是目前所大家工业界就是混合主力，它其实虽然从架构上来讲是一种Trade-off，但是从最后模型本身的表现来说并不是一个Trade-off。包括大家其实发现，在保持一定的全主力比例的基底上，整个模型其实是可以获得无损甚至是更好的一长上下单表现的。也就是说其实在混合注意力模型的架构中，我们并没有牺牲一定的全主力力的一个模型能力从从而带来工程收益。所以从这个角度上来说，也就是也是因为这个实验上的一个结论，然后从而导致这个混合注意力现在被大规模的利用起来。这是你的第一片工作是吧？对对对对，是的，是的。对，然后后来的话就是当时我们会发现说这个混合主力其实在工程上是一个%的Work的方法，我觉得其实没有问题，当然其实直到今天Work的也还是不错的嘛，当然对于一个博士上来讲就是这个东西就是虽然它比较Work大家也愿意去友，但是那这个东西从脑体上其实感觉就差一点意思，而且感觉也不够有趣，因为当时我们觉得就是如果只是把这两种混合主力模型结合起来的话，它有这么几个问题。我觉得最大的问题就是它在推理的加速比，它它永远是和这个混合比是正交的。或者说也不要正交，就是完全成正比例的。因为现在比如大家比较常用的是这个混合注意力比全注意力大家大概是3比1，这样的一种比例，也就是全主力在整个模型里大概是1/4这样的一种比例。所以说如果说我们认为就1/4是一个比较无损的也是一个比较极限的比例的话，其实我们很难拿到更大的加速比，也就是我们无论是Prescale还是做Decode的。我们的最大的加速比也就是四倍。当然我们在做纯线性注意力的时候，我们希望是更高的嘛，我们希望最后如果混合力它其实只是一个常数级别的改进，所以当时觉得可能不是很有意思，所以我们后来就做了Yoco这样的一种工作。对，当时我们的一个Motivation就是我们从第一性原理去思考的，为什么纯线性注意力没有办法获得和这个纯全注意力一样的性能。原因也很简单，就是我们至少希望模型有一些比较基本的能力，比如我们至少有一个，可以获取全全文信息的能力。所以说在这个场景下其实全上下单的KV-cache这一条是没有办法去省掉的。所以说这个KV-Cache既然没有省掉，对吧？我们能不能从另一个维度去省呢？所以当时我们就找到了另一条节省的方向，就是从这个层间这样去节省。所以Yoco当时做的是一个就是所有层共用一份KV-Cache这样的一种架构的方式。然后另外一个就是从模型的计算角度，就是每一个Token在decode的的过程当中，对它的计算密度是没有办法去这个减少的，因为我们希望它保持一定的全注意力的计算能力，然后这样它才能达到我们希望的长上下单能力。所以说我们提出了另一个东西，就是我们希望把这个模型的计算和存数结构开来，也就所以说我们当时可以看到就是虽然我们只有一份的KV-cache，但是我们可以保持一个多层的Full-attention的这样的一种计算结构，所以索在在这个基础上我们当时Yoco架构就可以就是我们只保留一份的KV-cache。但是可以获得这个和全注意力力或者混合注意力力基本上可以等价的一个模型的一个计算结果。然后这个架构还有另外一个优势就是我们在Prefill的时候可以直接跳过这个全注意力的计算。因为Prefill它就是（比如比如说也也就是模型处理这个用户输入的这样的一种阶段。我们本质上只是希望我们可以拿到模型的KV-cache，然后这个KV-cache是用于后期的这个Decode的的一系列的Hidden state。所以说这个Prefill的唯一目的就是去达到模型的KV-cache。所以说在这个这种架构上，我们经过前满的一些Linear-Attention的一个计算，然后拿到了KV-cache，这个就够了。因为Prefill阶段我们是没有必要就是解码出每一个位置的后期的Next Token Prediction的，所以在Prefill阶段我们就可以直接不过Cross-Attention。但是在Decode阶段我们可以保持和传统模型一样的一个计算能力。对，然后这个是我相关的一些第二片工作。这个标题也挺有意思的。对，这个也是致敬嘛，You Only Look Once。当然后来也有后面一系列的致敬的工作，这。你只能缓存一次是吧？呃，对，是的。对，这个其实在那个从KV-cache的存数角度基本上已经达到极致了。因为我们都同意对吧？如果从Token-wise角度我们必须得保存所有的的话，一份其实就是最少了，不可能再少了，所以这个从KV-cache角度是一个比较极限的一个结算。对，然后我讲我的Personally的我的第三篇工作，就是在这个工作中我们其实解决了，就是从一个更通用的角度来去考虑问题的话，就是模型的推理，开销主要就分为三个部分，就是Prefill的这个时间开销，然后Decode的开销，和KV-cache的存数改程。这三个是最主要的模型推理瓶颈，不可能有第四个。然后Yoco这个工作呢，我们主要解决了Prefill和解决了KV-cache，但是我们没有解决Decode。然后Decode的话我们后序是或者大家会尝试用这个Sparse Attention的方式去解决，然后但是K3它其实目前是没有用Sparse Attention，所以这块对今天就对今天就先不讲了。对，然后我们后满对于一个工作是一个和另一个赛道是一个比较相似的工作，也就是Loop Language Model。Loop Language Model有一个这个顾名思义也就是我们在保持一个固定的模型参数大小的情况下，去通过多次迭代，来去提升模型的Flops，从而去提升模型的总的一个这个能力。所以Loop这一系列的工作主要目的就是可以在保持一个比较小的模型参数的技术上，然后取得比同参数的模型好更多的一个效果。但是其实传统的这种Loop Language Model，它在Decode的情况下，它的成本其实是难以忍受的。因为它只是省了模型参数，它其实并没有省计算，而且在相同计算下的话，它其实是并不如把参数展开或者说用一个更大的但不去Loop的模型，它最后总的效果好。比较传统的Loop Language Model计算还有一个问题就是，它的这个推理，它的这个KV-cache，它的存数，它会随着这个模型推理的深度而增加。所以说最后总的算起来的Loop Language Model所带来的总的KV-cache其实是相当大的。然后后来Loop Universal的这个工作，其实就是在这个Yoco架构的基础上的，然后去作一个位置上的改变，也就是我们把Loop的单原然后变成一个很Efficient的一个单原，然后从而去避免就是推理的时候一些额外的或者是比较大的成本，但是能保持它模型的一个效果比较稳定地一个增长。解法也很简单，因为对于Yoco而言它毫无疑问它是一个混合注意力力架构，但是我们混合注意力方式它是它不是一个交错式的的一个结构，它是一个两端的一个结构，也就是我们模型推理的前夕它是一个全线性注意力力，但是模型后夕是一个全注意力力这样一个架构。所以说我们的选择是把Yoco这样的一种架构只集中在这个线性注意力力这样的一种位置。那这样的话我们对整个KV-cache增长是比较小的，因为Linear-Attention它本身的KV-cache它其实是微乎其微的。然后第二个就是线性主力它不仅省存数它其实也是省计算的，所以在这个长上下文中这样的一种场景下，它其实带来的额外的计算是相当小的。但是虽然它Linear-Attention所这个带来的Attention的Decode的计算它是相当小的，但是从General的模型能力上的话，它从比如说是模型Perplexity或者说从模型的Scaling behavior它其实是可以获得足够接近全注意力力的一个模型的模型能力。所以说我们我们这个架构它其实可以达到了一个效果就是从用用差不多这个比如说我们用两倍于原模型的这样一种计算强度，就我们基本上就可以获得两倍左右的一个性能提升，但是我们的模型存数和这个模型的KV-cache，还是保持和原模型的一个差不多这样的一种推理开销。对，这个是我第三篇工作，然后这篇工作因为去年开始这个Loop DHH: Future of Programming, AI, Agentic Engineering, Vibe Coding & Linux | Lex Fridman Podcast #501

Lex Fridman(5.0M)·315:51·609.4K views·2026-08-26
David Heinemeier HanssonCTO, 37signals Lex FridmanHost, Lex Fridman Podcast
TL;DRAI agent acceleration (particularly since late 2025/early 2026 models like Opus 4.5 and Fable) has fundamentally transformed programming from manual code-chiseling into high-level human vision, product management, and multi-threaded agent coordination.
DHH argues that this new...

TL;DR

AI agent acceleration (particularly since late 2025/early 2026 models like Opus 4.5 and Fable) has fundamentally transformed programming from manual code-chiseling into high-level human vision, product management, and multi-threaded agent coordination. DHH argues that this new "agent-accelerated" era has unlocked unprecedented productivity, enabling single developers to build and maintain complex software systems—such as his new Linux distro, Omarchy Quattro—at lightning speed.

The Agentic Revolution & Evolution of Programming

  • Shift to Agentic Acceleration: DHH describes moving from the pre-agentic era (where AI served merely as autocomplete or a chatbot sidekick) to the current "agentic" era, where models instrument computers, run verification tests, and execute complete tasks autonomously.
  • The 100% AI-Generated Linux Distro:
    • Over the last three months, DHH built Omarchy Quattro (an opinionated, modern Arch Linux distribution centered on the Hyprland Wayland tiling compositor) with nearly 100% agent-written code.
    • He estimates he has not written a single line of shipped code by hand for Quattro, instead acting purely as a reviewer, architect, and director.
  • Redefining "Vibe Coding" vs. Programming: While raw vibe coding (instructing an agent without looking at output) can destroy architecture in existing massive codebases, DHH argues that median human programmers often produce "slop" anyway, and agents follow explicit structural instructions much more diligently.
  • Becoming a Polyglot Programmer: Thanks to AI agents, DHH—traditionally a dedicated Ruby/Bash developer—has recently written functional applications in C++ (such as *Omawrite*, his custom Markdown writing app replacing Typora) and Rust (such as *TTFX*, a 10x faster translation of a Python terminal effects library).

Omarchy Quattro & the Philosophy of Extreme Speed

  • Obsession with Sub-Minute Installs: DHH pushed Omarchy Quattro’s installation time down from standard sluggish benchmarks (like a 42-minute macOS update or 1.5-hour Windows setup) to under 45 seconds (with a target of 12 seconds for optimized hardware), driven by the principle that "the pursuit of excellence deserves no explanation."
  • The "Omakase" Software Philosophy: Omarchy is heavily opinionated ("omakase" meaning chef's choice), shipping pre-configured with a curated suite of developer tools, a custom video clip editor (*Omacut*), Neovim, terminal utilities (TUI), and a plugin marketplace that gained over 330 community plugins in three days.
  • Terminal Renaissance: DHH champions Terminal User Interfaces (TUIs) and multi-threaded setups (using tools like `tmux` and `Herdr` across a cluster of mini-PCs via Tailscale WireGuard networks) as the ultimate high-bandwidth development environment.

Model Benchmarks & Agentic Workflows

  • Model Rankings & Cost/Performance:
    • Fable: Ranked by DHH as the overall best model due to its exceptional planning capabilities, though it commands a higher token cost (~$550 for translating a major Python library to Rust).
    • Claude Opus 5: A close second for implementation speed and quality.
    • GPT Sol & Grok 4.6: Highly capable frontier models performing complex translation and agentic tasks at roughly 1/10th the cost.
  • Multi-Agent Collaboration: DHH's standard operating procedure involves using Fable or Opus to draft plans and execute code, then routing through Codex, Grok, or GitHub Copilot for automated code review, bug hunting, and security auditing.

Societal Impact, Anxiety, and the Human Element

  • Empathy for Programmer Anxiety: DHH acknowledges the deep anxiety felt by computer science graduates and developers whose mechanical code-writing skills are being automated, noting that survival requires pivoting to product vision, taste, and system architecture.
  • The Jevons Paradox in Software: Lowering the cost of writing code dramatically increases the demand for software, creating a massive expansion in builder capacity rather than mass obsolescence.
  • Stoicism and *Amor Fati*: Urges developers to stop wasting energy trying to predict model hops two years out ("AI psychosis") and instead embrace the present moment with radical optimism.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Linux Desktop Domination: DHH predicts that Linux will eventually conquer the desktop space because its CLI-first, open config file structure is natively optimized for AI agents to diagnose errors, write patches, and execute system automation.
  • Malleable Operating Systems: DHH forecasts a future where users no longer interact with locked-down OS environments (like standard macOS/Windows) but instead "vibe code" and customize their operating systems on the fly using natural language.

Notable Quotes

*"There are decades where nothing happens and weeks where decades happen. And we have seen decades of progress happen in the last nine months."* — DHH
*"The Overton window does not open itself. It opens one nudge at a time by people risking a little. A little reputation, a little pushback, a little criticism..."* — DHH
*"The pursuit of excellence deserves no explanation. Wanting to make something as good and as fast and as beautiful as possible has no need for justification."* — DHH
There are decades where nothing happens and weeks where decades happen. And we have seen decades of progress happen in the last nine months. If you're not recognizing the gravity of the moment, that's the delusion; that's the psychosis. Who would not get delirious if suddenly a genie pops out of the bottle and says, "You can have whatever you want. Every feature you've ever dreamed of in an operating system, I can deliver them to you, most of them in five minutes, a few in 20, and if we really go hog wild, it's gonna take me two hours." I want the fastest car breaking the speed limits. I want the diver watch that can go down the Mariana Trench. I want the operating system that can install in less than 60 seconds. One of the things I always loved about race cars was when I would stumble out of the car absolutely smashed and barely able to hold my head up, and I'd lay down on the garage floor and just think, "Holy fuck, I'm alive." The Overton window does not open itself. It opens one nudge at a time by people risking a little—a little reputation, a little pushback, a little criticism, or maybe sometimes a lot of reputation or a lot of criticism or a lot of pushback. Creating life with another human that you love is literally the peak experience of being on the planet. The following is a conversation with David Heinemeier Hansson, also known as DHH, creator of Ruby on Rails, CTO of 37signals, creator of the new Omarchy Linux operating system, best-selling author, race car driver, and one of the most outspoken and prolific programmers in the world. For the past 20-plus years, for him, programming meant meticulously handcrafting beautiful Ruby code. But recently, since late 2025, DHH has embraced the AI revolution and has openly transformed himself into, once again, one of the most outspoken and prolific agentic engineers, even though he hates that term. So let's say practitioners of whatever programming is becoming where AI is doing most of the actual programming and the human steers the ship with high-level design, vision, and taste. Yes, it's true. DHH is at times controversial, but he's always fearless, brilliant, and fun to talk to. This was, once again, an intense, eye-opening, and wild rollercoaster ride of a conversation. This is a Lex Fridman podcast. To support it, please check out our sponsors in the description where you can also find links to contact me, ask questions, give feedback, and so on. And now, dear friends, here's DHH. 13 months ago, we sat down right here to talk about programming, and then everything changed. At that time, you were a bit skeptical about the role of AI in the process of programming, and then we went through this rapid evolution of agentic engineering. So, simple question to start: How has your view on AI's role in programming changed? Are you excited? Are you terrified? Are you on an emotional rollercoaster ride? I am incredibly excited. There is none of the existential threat. That doesn't exist for me as an emotional component. It is only there as an intellectual component, and the emotional component for me is 100% pure, unadulterated joy, optimism, and amazement that we've made computers do this. And I find it so interesting that we talked just 13 months ago because it's like we talked in different universes, different eras. I love this quote. I think it's Lenin: There are decades where nothing happens and weeks where decades happen. And we have seen decades of progress happen in the last nine months. I mean, imagine you're there when the Wright brothers take flight. Yesterday, the New York Times would write, "It's gonna be 10,000 years before we fly," and then the day after, we're up in the skies, and just a few years after that, there were cross-Atlantic planes. The whole world has completely changed. What a blessing to be there in that moment. If you zoom out and look at all of human history, how many humans got to live within the same epoch that they were born in? They never saw that complete change of the world and of society. And to have been blessed with two of those feels just such a privilege. I got to see the internet from pre-internet to post-internet, and then now pre-AI, post-AI. What an amazing run. How fortunate. Yeah, but the thing is, this feels like a thing that happened faster than anything else in human history. Correct. So I would say somewhere around December, maybe late November—November 24th. That's the exact moment. You know, when they talk about when one nation invades another in a world war or something like this, this is how we talk about AI changing everything. It really just shifted for many great developers. It shifted to where AI is doing some basic autocomplete, maybe writing 5, 10, 15, 20% of code, to writing 80% of code. Or 100%, yeah, especially if it's not a public-facing product. To me, that's why even when I look back upon our conversation a year ago, I don't actually have different opinions. I have the same opinions. A year ago, I did not like the mode of AI we were offered. It was the autocomplete mode, or it was the AI chatbot mode. Now, the chatbot I actually liked, as we talked about. Great tutor right from the get-go, great way of looking things up on the internet, not what was gonna replace me chiseling code. But then we get the agents. And the agents start out being curiosities for about five minutes, and then they get amazing, and then they get, "Oh my God, is this AGI?" And all of that happened since last year, even just within the last nine months. We have basically these few phases here. We have AI in the pre-agentic era. I was excited about that, but it was not fundamentally rewriting the rules of the game for me. It was not completely changing how I worked. I was still chiseling code, I just had a little helper, a little sidekick who could bounce ideas off, and I could look up this information online and so forth in a more efficient way. It was just a more efficient way to do what I was already doing, and it didn't change the emotional connection I had to the computer. Then we get to November 24th, 2025. Opus 4.5, to me, was the dividing line, where suddenly... I didn't even try it on the 24th. I think I tried it on the 26th. I give it a couple of tasks, and I realize that the quality of the output is uncannily close to what I would've written. And I remember just leaning back and thinking, "What just happened? How did we go from this autocomplete mess that I was talking to you about in the summer to this just a few short months later? How did we get both the increase in intelligence and then also the increase in usability?" This agent harness question where I don't know if Opus 4.5 was that much smarter than Opus 4, which is what we had in the summer, but its ability to instrument your computer, to use tools, to check its own work, to apply its intelligence in such a way that you could get real meaningful work out of it, was completely different. And I think this is then the big change that happens for almost anyone who paid attention and started playing with it over the Christmas break. This is what I heard from Shopify and other places with lots of employees who suddenly had a breather, suddenly had a couple of weeks to lean back and just look at what was going on, gave it a try, and had the same mind-blowing experience that these agents were of a different genre than what we had before. And then what we get to is, I'm already excited at this point. Like, by December, I'm already revisiting sort of all my priors and going like, "Wow, if it can do this, can it also do that? Oh, yeah, it can." And again, Opus 4.5 now looks like a retarded model. And this is the magic of this progress, is you think you've reached something. I remember thinking at the moment, "If this is the last model we get, I'll be set. I'll be happy." I could live with Opus 4.5 for the next 20 years and you would hear no complaints from me because it was just so incredibly amazing to see an agent do all this work in the way that I wanted it done. Because it was not just about it being able to solve a task, it was also that I could look at its path there and go, "Yep. Yep. Maybe not there, but almost," and it would give you two notes. You'll get to where I wanted to go in the way I wanted to go there. You could produce code I wanted to merge. You could produce code that actually looked beautiful if it was written in Ruby. Rust, different question. But this ability for the agents to truly become an extension of how I wanted to work was very novel. But then we wait just until early spring, and suddenly we get sub-agents. We get harnesses that can subdivide the task, and something that would take Opus quite a while suddenly took a fifth of the time, a tenth of the time, because it could get chopped up and suddenly you got eight sub-agents working for you. But both of those two first phases of the agentic age, to me, still felt like I had to drive. I could tell it what I wanted, where to look for it, and steer it a little bit when it went off, and then we'll get there, and I would go much faster. But I had to be in the driver's seat. I had to tell it what I wanted, and I had to be the reviewer, the auditor of what was coming out. And then finally now, this summer, with Opus 5, Fable, and Sol, GPT Sol, and to a lesser extent, some of the open weight models, we've arrived at a new era where I'm not telling it where we're going. I'm telling it the problem I have. I'm telling it the fuzzy, vague idea I have. It tells me where we're going. It tells me which path to take, and I will still look at it because I'm a curious person and I like computers and I like the outcome of it, but I really kinda don't have to. I've become optional in the part that produces the code that picks the route. Remember when early GPS systems came out? Actually, to push back against myself, I do find the actual skill I'm currently developing, which I along those lines is probably what everybody's trying to develop, is not to overspecify things; it is to get out of the system's way. Of course. It is to specify enough to provide a kind of high-level vision and guidance. It's even better because you don't even need that. The fundamental insight of modern software development came from the agile software development movement. In the late '90s, early 2000s, a group of smart, honest, and brave people came together and said, "The way we've been trying to do software development for the last 40 to 50 years has not worked, will not work. We've been trying to specify upfront what software should look like. We thought that we could ask humans what they wanted, write it all down, apply our systems thinking and our rigor to a spec sheet, hand that spec sheet over to a group of programmers who would then implement just that, and everyone would be happy." Well, guess what? No one was happy. Because no one knows what they want until they receive it. You don't know what a program should do until you play with it. So in the agentic age, you should resist the temptation to be overly specific upfront. Be as vague as you can to manifest something, then interact with the something. The way you arrive at good software is you write a little bit of software, and then you try to use it. It is in the process of using software that you discover what you really want. It's where you discover what's important and what's not important. And agents are incredible at allowing you to bumble into this experience of realizing what you want because you don't know what you want. Mm-hmm. I think this, by the way, is one of the other breakthroughs that is going to lead to the total domination of Linux. The agents have taken all the hardship out of diagnosing Linux systems and turned the fact that Linux produces these overly specific, totally arcane error messages into its greatest advantage. When Linux has an issue, the agent can take that very specific error message that makes no sense to a normal human and correlate it with the fact that the agent was pre-trained on 40 million lines of Linux code, so it knows exactly where to look and dial it down. Then offers you whether you wanna file a bug report with all this detail. I'll give you one amazing anecdote. So I was working with this guy JDX, who's working on mise. This, this package manager for fast-moving development tools. This is how we manage all the agent software and so forth. Normal package managers are not built to be updated seven times a day. And all of these agent harnesses are updated about seven times a day, so we needed an out-of-band package manager, and mise just turns out to be perfect for this. Anyway, it had a small bug. There was some race condition which agents are very good at finding because when you start running multiple agents at the same time, they will suss out all these race conditions you have in your underlying infrastructure that was never triggered by a human trying to do manual thing. So it finds this issue, right? I tell the agent, "Hey, can you post this as a bug report to JDX's GitHub?" And unfortunately, right before that, I had had to do a QA run on Omarchy itself with eight different agents. It had found 28 real issues that it needed to file. Well, it went to GitHub and tried to file all 28 issues at the same time, which it did in about, I don't know, 12 seconds. GitHub, not unreasonably, marked that as probable spam and banned my Omarchy bot. And then I couldn't do that. So I was blocked from the bot having access to GitHub, so I just told it, "Hey, do you know what? Just email JDX. Here's his email address." I had already set it up with hey.com an email address. We have a CLI that's in beta right now. So I set it up with that so it can send to email. That's how it gives me reports about outstanding issues and PRs. So it sends JDX this email about the bug it found, and he was like, "Wait, this is the first time I've gotten a bug report in unreleased software." Because it had downloaded the source code to mise, saw that he had already fixed the bug, but that it would not fix the problem entirely in software they had not shipped yet. Pinpointed the problem, and he was like, "Damn it, I got a bug report before we even cut a release." That's amazing. That's amazing, believable AGI levels of mind-blowing stuff. And that was actually, if you remember, that was an open question whether AI systems could debug well. Grok 4.6 I just started testing a few days ago. I had this wonderful test that I've set up by accident where I translated this Python library into Rust. The screensaver you just saw with all the cool animation? That's powered by a Python library called Terminal Text Effects. Really cool library. We've been using it since the first day of Omarchy. The problem with that is it's written in Python, so when it starts up, especially on a laptop, and it runs in Python, it uses all of your CPU to do these effects, and therefore, it means it uses about 30 watts of energy, and it spins up your fans, and it drains your battery. Doesn't really matter on a local computer, but it does matter on a laptop. So I thought, "Do you know what? This sounds like a problem for Rust." So first I gave Fable the challenge, and all I told it was, "Here's the source code for the Python library," this TTE library that had a bunch of dependencies and so forth. "I want a Rust version of this with no dependencies, a single executable." Like, that's what Rust does. So basically, I want it in Rust. I want it to be pixel perfect, frame by frame, do a full analysis, don't stop until you're finished. I kid you not, in just under 45 minutes, it was like, "I'm finished. I've checked everything. I have reduced the startup time from 86 milliseconds to two milliseconds. I have sped up the execution time by 9.6 times, I believe it was. The executable is three megabytes. Do you wanna run it?" So I run it— and I don't know why I'm surprised, because this translation job is something we've known for a while that AI is pretty good at, but it was still staggering to me that I could one-shot a full translation of a Python library I had been using for a year, that others had been using for much longer, and turn it into a Rust executable without knowing any Rust, without looking at the Rust code at all, and produce this executable that I then told the agent right after, "This is great. Ship it." It packaged it up as a new package. It told me, "What do you want it to call?" "Let's call it TTFX. Let's create a new Git repo." It sets up the Git repo. "Let's create a new package, build package for our build system." It puts that up. "Let's push it out. Let's open the pull request to the Omarchy itself so we switch around from TTE, the Python implementation, to TTFX." It does all of it, and I'm just sitting there. And again, I'm already at this point fully delirious with agent acceleration, and I still had to lean back and I go like, "This is AGI, isn't it? This is what AGI looks like. If we have... David Graeber wrote this wonderful piece, Bullshit Jobs, back in, I think it was maybe early 2010s. Based on a poll he did in the UK asking people, at that time, would... I think the question was something like, "Would it make a difference if you didn't go to work?" For humanity, for mankind. And something like 30-some percent answered, "No, it wouldn't." And this was my argument since day one with startups. This was why we stayed a small company. Glean:GO 2026 Keynote

Glean(8.2K)·108:58·3.9K views·2026-08-26
Arvind JainFounder and CEO, Glean Kate RooneyJournalist and Moderator
TL;DRGlean introduced new proactive AI capabilities, its enterprise AI platform features (including AI Gateway and Glean Tau), and advanced governance/threat detection tools to help enterprises move from simple question-answering to automated, multi-team workflows.
Retrieval and...

TL;DR

Glean introduced new proactive AI capabilities, its enterprise AI platform features (including AI Gateway and Glean Tau), and advanced governance/threat detection tools to help enterprises move from simple question-answering to automated, multi-team workflows.

Context Engine, AI Platform, Memory, and Model Choice

  • Retrieval and Enterprise Graph: Arvind Jain explained that Glean's context engine combines advanced retrieval (surfacing current, high-quality information) with an enterprise graph that automatically maps people, customers, teams, projects, and relationships across structured and unstructured data.
  • AI Gateway: Arvind Jain and Allen noted that the gateway makes disparate AI tools and front doors (like Microsoft Copilot, ChatGPT, and Cursor) think alike and work consistently while providing access to over 40 open and frontier models.
  • Memory Platform: Glean automatically learns employee working preferences and agent improvement over time, making this memory portable and shareable across all enterprise AI tools via the AI Gateway.
  • Token Efficiency and Auto-Routing: Tony and Eddie detailed Glean's auto-routing technology, which uses evaluation frameworks and specialist models (like Glean Waldo) to match tasks with the optimal model and reasoning level, saving 81% on token costs compared to Claude Cowork.
  • Glean Tau: Arvind Jain and Allen introduced Glean Tau, a desktop app combining an open-source coding harness with Glean's context platform and AI gateway for code generation and coworking.

Proactive AI, Independent Agents, and Teamchat

  • Proactive AI Features: Emrecan announced proactive AI capabilities that prioritize/execute task management, run email triage, power meeting coaches, and provide interactive dashboards.
  • Multiplayer AI (Teamchat): Enables teammates and Glean to collaborate inside a shared workspace based on shared context rather than single-player prompt loops.
  • Independent Agents: Long-running agents with distinct identities, memories, and governance that operate autonomously across teams (e.g., automatically detecting and fixing API failures in Slack or handling customer support).
  • Glean Transform: Emrecan presented this tool for mapping how work actually gets done, identifying transformation opportunities, building agent blueprints in one click, and measuring workflow improvements (such as a 46% increase in customer escalation speed).

Security, Governance, and Threat Detection

  • AI Protect and Guardrails: Allen and Blair discussed how Glean Protect handles sensitive content detection (at 95% accuracy), restricted topic policies, and agent governance policies at the individual agent level.
  • Context-Aware AI Threat Detection: Allen introduced this new feature to spot high-risk agentic behavior and data exfiltration across sessions by utilizing enterprise context to distinguish legitimate activity from insider threats.

Enterprise Transformations and Customer Case Studies

  • General Motors: Amar Maletira and Phil Lutka shared that GM scaled Glean adoption from zero to 60,000 users in 90 days, maintaining an 80%+ 5D30 sustained usage metric, and deployed design release engineer (DRE) agents to help engineers design cars faster.
  • Snap: Evan Spiegel noted that Snap uses Glean agents for quarterly performance reviews, IT self-service (Tech Lounge), and R&D tax credit documentation, saving thousands of hours and increasing automated advertising image reviews to nearly 90%.
  • Nvidia: Carrie stated that 90% of Nvidia's 42,000 employees use Glean daily for IT search and run over 400 custom Slack agents backed by Glean's enterprise-ready platform and Nvidia NemoTron models.
  • Snowflake: Vivek shared that Snowflake improved internal engineering NPS by a 16x swing in 18 months by treating engineers like customers and letting agentic chaos reign before reining it in.
  • DaVita: Madhusudan Narasimhan and Aaron Groh reported that 40,000 out of 70,000 clinic and office employees actively use Glean, generating 5.4 million queries and saving approximately 19–20 minutes per person to refocus on patient care.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Year of Open Source: "2026 is the year of open source in AI... open-source harnesses often beat close ones in benchmarks, and they're allowing us to create amazing AI products very quickly." — Arvind Jain

Notable Quotes

"Context is what transforms intelligence into relevance. The most critical part of getting the right and precise output from the model." — *Enterprise Experts (Video Montage)*
"Give people access to the right model for the right task: frontier models when they need it, small models when they don't, because if AI is going to scale across the enterprise, every token has to earn its keep." — *Michael (Finance Lead, Glean)*
"RAG is only one part of the challenge when you're building agents and agents are the entire system... when you put this all together, you still need connectors, permissions, governance, security, and compliance." — *Carrie (Nvidia)*

And to make that all happen at runtime, we've post trained our own specialist model, Glean Waldo, that's post trained on strong open source base models such as the Nemetron family, and that model is capable of routing to the right, reasoning level on demand. Yeah, and, you know, as an engineer, as a product builder, the part I really love is we didn't, we didn't win that benchmark on, on just this, this, choice, the price per token alone. We actually use 70% fewer tokens. Can you tell us why? Yeah, totally. All these things kind of compound for token efficiency, right? So it comes back to our roots in search. With search, we have a very purposeful choice to pre-compute a central index, build this enterprise graph that has all these signals. And that way the model can much more efficiently find that relevant context at runtime instead of, sort of, building it up incrementally, wasting a lot of loops collecting it, right? So, Claude Cowork and other harnesses, other setups might take a different approach. When they rely on federated MCPs, those services will end up searching live across a lot of sources and they all those different sources have different search qualities, they're not reconciled. All that data then needs to be deduplicated, filtered, joined, all while your tokens are burning, right? So, that's a pretty token intensive operation, and it shows up in this token difference. Yeah, and that's the cost side, right? But, token yield is about pairing costs and quality, that balance. Cheap, cheap and wrong would be worthless, right? So on quality, Glean was actually preferred 78% of the time over Claude Cowork. Thanks for that. And, and, the gold thing is that preference showed up showed up across every individual department as well, too. Yeah, so what these results come back to is the question of usability, right? How useful is this output in my actual work? Can I trust it? Is it correct? Is it complete? And a big reason that Glean was preferred comes back to our search foundations. So it allows us to pair high quality context with the right model for the task, not always the most expensive model, and that gives us significant gains in both quality and cost together. Yeah. and we actually, we got so excited about auto routing here that we made a quick video about it. So let's roll that. So we've learned a lot about tokens in the last year. My name is Michael and I lead finance at Glean. And for the last three years, we've been telling everyone the same thing: use AI, use a lot of it, TokenMax, build with it, put it into your workflow, and guess what? It worked. And they actually did use AI. In fact, they used a lot of it, and token costs went up, and the CFO took notice. And then we entered the Token Whiplash. Now we're all asking: how do we control the cost without killing the behavior we worked so hard to create? And the answer is in model choice: different models for different tasks. Because right now, too many employees are using frontier models to do non-frontier work. That means tasks that are simple are getting handed to a really expensive frontier model. Using a frontier model for a non-frontier task is like asking your CTO every time you need a reset of a password. Why is everyone asking me to reset their password? And also, speaking for CFOs, they aren't the villain here and this isn't less use of AI; it's just more responsible use of AI and smarter AI economics. Tokens are just dollars in another currency. Giving every employee unconstrained tokens is like giving every employee a credit card with no spend limit. That would be insane. Limits exist for a reason; they build responsible AI use, and they help enterprises stay in control. Give people access to the right model for the right task: frontier models when they need it, small models when they don't, because if AI is going to scale across the enterprise, every token has to earn its keep. TL;DRRocket Lab Founder & CEO Sir Peter Beck walked through the company's Long Beach headquarters, highlighting its evolution from a bootstrapped New Zealand startup into a vertically integrated, publicly traded space enterprise spanning launch, satellite manufacturing, and mission-critical components.

TL;DR

Rocket Lab Founder & CEO Sir Peter Beck walked through the company's Long Beach headquarters, highlighting its evolution from a bootstrapped New Zealand startup into a vertically integrated, publicly traded space enterprise spanning launch, satellite manufacturing, and mission-critical components.

Company Origins & Capital-Efficient Culture

  • Bootstrapped Beginnings: Founded in 2006 in New Zealand with a total capitalization under $100,000 NZD (~$60,000 USD).
  • Rutherford Engine Philosophy: Named after Ernest Rutherford's quote ("We have no money so we have to think"), utilizing resourcefulness and salvaged parts (like junkyard gas fittings) to overcome tight financial constraints.
  • Early Milestones:
    • Launched the first private suborbital rocket in the southern hemisphere (RT1) in 2009, just three years after inception.
    • Secured DARPA propellant and propulsion work in 2010.
    • Raised a $5 million Series A from Khosla Ventures in 2013, flipping the company into a US entity and enabling the first 3D-printed Rutherford engine hot-fire.
    • Closed a Bessemer-led B round in 2014, broke ground on Launch Complex 1 in New Zealand in 2015, finished it in 2016, launched their first vehicle in 2017, and achieved unicorn status with their D round.

Going Public & M&A Strategy

  • Rigorous Public Discipline: Went public in 2021 to enforce extreme financial discipline and build a multi-generational space company that persists beyond its founder, ensuring long-term impact.
  • Complex Acquisitions: Acquired three companies simultaneously in 2021—a painful and resource-intensive integration process that underscored the operational friction of M&A.
  • The Space Component Moat: While rockets capture public attention, owning the entire stack of spacecraft components (reaction wheels, buses, propulsion) serves as a massive competitive moat due to a fractured, constrained supply chain.

Facilities & Mission Capabilities

  • Global Footprint: Spans 13 acres of factories globally, including locations across the United States (Long Beach, Virginia, Albuquerque, Tucson, Middle River for Neutron rockets, Stennis), Canada (Toronto), Germany (Manarak), and New Zealand.
  • Long Beach Facility: Encompasses ~800 employees across two campuses, housing executive offices, satellite manufacturing clean rooms, component testing, and a mission control center.
  • High-Profile Missions:
    • CAPSTONE Moon Mission: Controlled out of Long Beach and New Zealand, testing a stable gateway lunar orbit for NASA.
    • ESCAPADE: Mars-bound spacecraft currently managed out of the Long Beach mission control.
    • LOxSAT: A NASA program building a liquid oxygen storage sphere on orbit for long-duration interplanetary missions.
    • Venus Life Detection Mission: A conceptual and ongoing scientific pursuit to probe Venus's cloud layer for phosphine gas and potential organic life.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • *No explicit forward-looking predictions, timelines, or quantitative targets with stated conditions were stated by the speaker in this transcript excerpt.*

Reverse-Engineering the AI Buyer — Aliisa Rosenthal, Acrew Capital

AI Engineer(627.0K)·19:10·686 views·2026-08-26
Aliisa RosenthalPartner, Acrew Capital
TL;DR- **Early Growth Metrics:** Aliisa Rosenthal joined OpenAI a little over four years ago when revenue was at a couple million and powered by a self-serve API for developers; she helped scale the organization to several hundred people and several billion in enterprise revenue.
OpenAI grew enterprise revenue to several billion dollars by doing the go-to-market sequence backward—building an expensive enterprise tier before self-serve, which cannibalized sales. Founders should invert this: automate the go-to-market machine first using modern...

TL;DR: OpenAI grew enterprise revenue to several billion dollars by doing the go-to-market sequence backward—building an expensive enterprise tier before self-serve, which cannibalized sales. Founders should invert this: automate the go-to-market machine first using modern tools, launch self-serve upfront, avoid pilots, and only hire humans (starting with AI-native talent) to solve specific bottlenecks.

1. Go-to-Market Sequencing & The OpenAI Origin Story

  • Early Growth Metrics: Aliisa Rosenthal joined OpenAI a little over four years ago when revenue was at a couple million and powered by a self-serve API for developers; she helped scale the organization to several hundred people and several billion in enterprise revenue.
  • The Backward Enterprise Rollout:
      • Launched ChatGPT in late 2022 with zero enterprise features (no SSO, NDA, or invoicing).
      • Begged the technical team for enterprise features for 9 months while large enterprises bombarded the sales team with inbound requests.
      • Shipped an expensive, fast, high-end enterprise product targeting up-market buyers first.
  • The Cannibalization Lesson: Shipped a self-serve version 4 months later in January 2024; it grew exponentially faster, cannibalized the enterprise tier, frustrated sales reps who had to compete with self-serve, and proved that mid-market/SMB self-serve was higher-growth than heavy enterprise.
  • Corrected Playbook: For all subsequent OpenAI product launches, the team enforced a self-serve first motion, learned what features were missing, and only then tacked on human-led enterprise tiers.

2. Automating the Go-to-Market Machine

  • "Build the Machine First": Traditional sales leaders hire large teams (BDRs, SDRs, RevOps, sales engineers) and augment with AI later; founders should do the exact opposite by automating go-to-market workflows first and adding humans only at bottlenecks.
  • Inbound Management & Data Capture:
      • OpenAI faced a tidal wave of 10,000 inbounds per day with just 4 sales reps.
      • Signup Form Optimization: Add in-depth fields (like phone numbers, even if optional) to enable future AI or human outreach.
      • Automated Acknowledgment: Send immediate automated waitlist/feedback emails to inbound prospects so they feel heard; missing this at OpenAI led prospects to buy Microsoft Copilot instead.
  • Outbound Automation: Leverage modern tooling (like Clay and Nooks) to automate research, sequencing, and dialing so reps focus exclusively on qualified leads and high-value conversations.

3. Removing Buyer Friction & Eliminating Pilots

  • Ease the Buyer's Journey: Never give buyers homework or tell them to "go do five things and come back"—do the heavy lifting via live Zoom sessions, in-office hackathons, or direct hand-holding to maintain control of the sales cycle.
  • Streamlined Buying Process: Keep pricing options simple, paperwork fast, and approval flows manageable.
  • Avoiding "Pilot Hell":
      • Treat pilots/POCs as rare exceptions for high-value revenue opportunities rather than the default, because they drain resources and require restarting the sales cycle.
      • Pilot Alternatives: Bypass POC objections by introducing them to another customer, running evaluations on part of their data via Zoom, offering a 90-day opt-out clause in a signed contract, or writing a 1-year POC instead of a standard 3-year agreement.
  • Security Automation: Combat security bottlenecks by building trust portals where buyers can auto-sign NDAs, access pen tests, and use AI tools to auto-fill security questionnaires.

4. Pricing Evolution

  • The Seat-Pricing Mistake: ChatGPT Enterprise launched at $60 per user per month (priced based on serving costs before market comparables like Copilot, Gemini, and Anthropic existed).
  • The Seat Ceiling: High per-seat pricing restricted adoption, as companies only purchased licenses for narrow subsets of teams (e.g., developers or investors) rather than whole companies.
  • The Usage-Based Pivot: OpenAI lowered the barrier to entry by shifting to a flat base license fee coupled with usage-based pricing, causing adoption to spread like wildfire.
  • Controlling Usage Costs: Counter customer fears of spiraling usage bills by providing spending cap dashboards per month or per employee.

5. Hiring Humans and Compensation

  • Timing the Human Layer: Hire humans only when hitting ceilings in automated processes where buyers explicitly demand human contact, trust-building, or up-market navigation.
  • Resisting Premature Upmarket Pull: Avoid chasing massive enterprise deals ($1M to $10M) too early, as they consume disproportionate legal, security, product, engineering, and sales resources.
  • Profile of Early Sales Hires: Hire AI-native account executives who live and breathe AI, practice what they preach, and are motivated by equity and building the company rather than W-2 cash.
  • The "Revenge of the Steak Dinner": As automation handles outbound, demos, and security, buyers increasingly crave in-person interaction; effective, low-cost human-led strategies include conferences, booths, and dinners.
  • Compensation Plans: OpenAI operated for years without giving out formal commission comp plans (relying on equity and company momentum), but founders eventually need variable comp to attract coin-operated enterprise sellers. Simpler comp structures with out-performer upside work best.

6. Q&A Insights: PLG vs. POCs, FDEs, and Design Partners

  • PLG Access vs. POCs: Product-led growth (self-serve access) is distinct from high-touch POCs; PLG funnels can be safely feature-gated, time-boxed, or consumption-limited without requiring heavy sales engineering support.
  • Forward-Deployed Engineers (FDEs):
      • FDEs are exceptionally difficult to hire (noting roughly 55,000 open job requisitions against a talent pool of about 5,000 qualified people).
      • While expensive and scarce, FDEs make products deeply sticky because customized integrations bind the customer to the tool long-term.
  • First 10 Design Partners:
      • Early design partner engagements should rely on existing relationships, investor introductions, or warm connections.
      • Target companies with strong logos by having them pilot the product on internal, non-production workflows to bypass heavy security hurdles while securing a valuable reference logo.

Notable Quotes

"My current advice to founders is do the opposite... start with what you can automate... build the machine first before you build the team." — Aliisa Rosenthal

"Almost every company on the list had the same answer: you never got back to me, so I bought Microsoft Copilot." — Aliisa Rosenthal
"You're not selling your tool. You're not selling your product. You're selling what that tool or that product does to your champion or your buyer." — Aliisa Rosenthal
Hi everyone. My name is Alyssa and I joined a little research lab called OpenAI a little over four years ago. When I joined it was you know a couple million in revenue. We had a self-serve API. We had some developers who found it and swiped a credit card and started building. Over the next few years I helped grow the organization to several hundred people, grow our revenue to several billion on the enterprise side and and launched multiple products. So I really had the opportunity to be at the bleeding edge of bringing this somewhat alien technology to organizations from startups to massive enterprises and I'm going to share a little bit about what I learned along the way. So I think most organizations typically have said okay, I'm ready to build out my go-to-market organization. I'm going to hire a bunch of sales people. I'm going to hire RevOps. I'm going to hire sales engineers and I'm going to build out my sales organization and then I'm going to figure out where the bottlenecks are and I'm going to add AI or I'm going to add automation to figure out how to scale this thing. My current advice to founders is do the opposite. So start with what you can automate. Start with there's so many tools available now in 2026 for a modern go-to-market or sales organization that have never been available before which really turns the entire playbook on its head. And you'll find that a lot of traditional sales leaders are very used to coming in and hiring a really big team and augmenting everything with humans, bringing in sales operations, sales engineers, lots of sales people, BDRs, SDRs. You can do so much of this through an automated approach now. So my my advice to founders is build the machine first before you build the team. Figure out what you can automate. Figure out where the bottlenecks are and what's broken and then add humans on top of that to get through those bottlenecks. So, just a story about how this played out at OpenAI. So, we launched ChatGPT in the end of 2022. When we launched it, we had no enterprise features whatsoever. And I got bombarded with organizations saying, "Hey, everyone in my company is using ChatGPT. Can I get SSO? Can I get an NDA? Can I get an invoice?" I had none of these things. I had no enterprise features whatsoever. So, over the next few months, I begged and begged and begged our technical team, "Can we build an enterprise version of this product and we build some enterprise features?" And finally, 9 months later, we got approval. Now, over that 9-month time, the companies that had been the loudest, the companies that had gotten to me, were the bigger companies, were the large enterprises. So, I thought, "Okay, we should go out and build a really high-end product for all these enterprises that want to use us." So, we added every bell and whistle. We made it lightning fast. It was a very expensive product that we shipped. We went up market. We went way up market right out the gate. And, you know, we did fine. We We did pretty well. You know, and and I think for most companies we did great, but after about a quarter, we said, "Well, we should probably also offer a self-serve version. Not everyone wants to opt into this heavy sales process and start with these really high price points." So, then we released our self-serve motion in January of 2024, so about 4 months after our enterprise version, and it just completely cannibalized our enterprise business. It grew so much faster. It turns out most people just didn't want to talk to a salesperson, and our mid-market SMB sort of self-serve business was much higher growth than our and then our heavy enterprise business. So, it frustrated the sales reps because now they had to compete with self-serve. It sort of cannibalized the customer base. They sometimes they preferred to just move into the cheaper self-serve option. And so, that was a really valuable lesson. We should have launched with self-serve first, listen to feedback from our customers and what they weren't getting from the self-serve product, and then figured out how to add build a more expensive enterprise offering, and then hire the team to sell it. So, we did it all backwards. It was a really really valuable learning experience, and from there we decided not to do that again. For every other product we launched at OpenAI, we made sure to to do it self-serve first, learn where we needed to tack on humans and add them. The other thing I'll mention, and you know, if Everett's still here, nice shout-out to him. We used Clay at OpenAI, and it was really really useful for us. So, in the beginning, we were just completely overwhelmed with inbound, which I know sounds like such a champagne problem, but it was me and four sales sales reps, and we were getting, you know, 10,000 inbound a day. I mean, it was just the most staggering problem to deal with this tidal wave of inbound. And, you know, if I could go back in time, there's so much I would have done differently. I would have, first of all, added way more fields to our sign-up form, like phone number, because eventually when we did catch up and we built all the automation and we figured out how to handle these leads, I didn't have a lot of information to go follow up with people. So, add a very in-depth sales form. It can be optional, but if people are willing to give you their phone number, you should get it, because at some point you might want to call them or have AI call them. So, gather as much info as you can. Try to respond to everyone, and I I you know, I wish I would have done this, at least just sent some email out saying, "Hey, we hear you. We don't have our enterprise product ready yet. You're on our waitlist. You're on our list. Tell us XYZ about what you're looking for." Just some sort of automated email campaign to make them feel like we cared. Because when we finally did launch our enterprise product 9 months later, almost every company we went out and talked to said, "Well, you never got back to me." And I went out and I brought Microsoft Copilot. So, that was a that was a really hard lesson. And then similarly on outbound, you know, there's lots of great tools out there now. Clay is one of them. I hear really great things about Nooks. There are really wonderful tools to start researching, sequencing, dialing, and automate as much as the outbound pipeline gen as you can, so your reps can focus on the qualified leads and the high value conversations. So, this is my biggest advice to any company starting to build their go-to-market motion is just make it easy on your buyers. Don't give them homework. Don't make them go and fill out a form or if you can avoid it, integrate something without you hand-holding them through that. Never say, "Hey, go do these five things and then come back to me." or "Go ask this person about this and come back to me." You do as much of the work for them. You hand-hold them through that. Maybe that means you have to set up hackathons and come in their office and do it with them in person. Maybe that means you have to set up some sort of Zoom session and do it live with them. But, whatever you do, don't send your buyer away to do something and wait for them to come back because you lose total control of the sales cycle and it makes everything take longer. Secondly, remove as much friction as you can in the buying process. Fewer options is better on pricing. Make the paperwork easy and fast. Make the approval flow as manageable as possible. We've already talked about investing in self-service, doing that up front before you add on humans. And the other thing I'll hammer home is to avoid pilots at all cost. And this is something that comes up when I talk to founders a lot is they say, "We are stuck in pilot hell. We've got all of these pilots going on. None of them are converting. They're so much work." Or if they do convert, it just I have to run an entire sales process all over again. I sell the pilot. I invest really heavily in managing the pilot. And then I have to start all over again from scratch once I get a yes to move forward on the sales cycle. So, use pilots sparingly. They should be not the default, but the option for your most valuable, highest paying, biggest revenue opportunities. Now, how do you get out of a pilot? Because every customer will ask you for one. They'll say, "I need to try this out. I need a POC. It's part of our process. I need to validate this works in my environment. It meets my needs. We have, you know, all of these things we need to validate and check on." Just remember, as soon as you give someone access to your product, you're giving away a lot of power and leverage in the deal cycle. So, there's lots of ways to get around these objections without a pilot. You can introduce them to another customer. You can do an eval on on part of their data. You can do a demo with their custom data and you do it over Zoom rather than handing them access to it. You can give them an opt-out clause. It's not something you do when you're a giant company, but when you're a startup, you can get away with these things. So, sign a contract, you have 90 days to opt out if it doesn't meet your needs, and that shifts the onus of trying out the product and validating it onto them because the clock is ticking on them now. So, it gives you a lot more control over the deal. And then another one I like to do is I say, "Well, our normal contracts are 3 years, but we'll do a 1-year POC with you." So, there's all kinds of ways to sort of negotiate your way around a POC without putting tons of resources and getting stuck in them. The other thing is security. Security is where deals tend to stall out and die over time. There's a lot you can automate now. There's so many wonderful tools on the market to build these trust portals where, you know, companies can come in and they can auto sign an NDA. They can self-serve for your pen test and security documentation. There's AI products that can auto fill out your security questionnaires. So, automate as much as the security as you can, and most companies will tell you, "Oh, I need to get on a call. I need to talk for 2 hours." Push back on that. You know, push them towards your trust portal and then make them come to you and say, "Here, you know, here's what I couldn't find. Here's what wasn't available. Here's why I need a call." And try to automate security because a lot of it really can be automated now, which really, really speeds up the sales cycle. So, another thing we learned, another expensive story, and this goes back to sort of building the Porsche before the Prius, is we built this this really high-end product, this enterprise product, and we set the price really high. We set it initially ChatGPT Enterprise was $60 per user per month. Now, we were really the first ones on the market, and we didn't know what pricing was going, so we we basically priced it according to how expensive it was for us to serve it. And then all the other products came out on the market, Copilot and Gemini and Anthropic at a much lower price point. And we realized we had set the price too high. So, we eventually went and we did lower the price. And what we did, we changed the model to basically a license fee, a base fee to use the product and then move to usage based. And then we just saw the barrier to entry went down. So, we had so many more companies signing licenses and signing contracts with us. And then usage went up over time as more as we started the barrier to entry went down. Because when it was $60 per month, organizations would come in and say, well, I'm only buying this for a subset of my team or only for my developers or only for my investors. I'm not going to buy it for my whole company. It's too expensive. But once we lowered the threshold, it spread like wildfire. Everyone started using it and it was a much better pricing model that we learned the hard way. I know over time now seats are out. This has become common knowledge. We learned it the hard way. And I also know that now usage is also getting pushed back as companies see cost of usage skyrocket. There's easy ways around this. So, you could for example, cap. You can say, hey, we have a dashboard. You can cap your spend per month. Most companies don't end up using that, but they like to know that it's there. You can cap certain employees spend. But people like the idea that it's a low barrier to entry. People can use it. If they're using it, ideally they're getting value out of it. And then there's ways to handle the objections of what if the cost spirals out of control. All right. So, we've talked about building the machine. When do you hire the humans? When do you start bringing on sales people? Sales people are expensive. You know, that's a big hire. How do you know when the time is right? It's basically when you're starting to hit you've gone as far as you can in your sales process and you're really having a lot of companies saying, I just need to talk to somebody whether it's I need a demo. I just need to look someone in the eye and build the trust here. And you're going to have that as you start to move up market naturally. Bigger companies are going to want to shake the hands of a human and have a conversation with them. On that note, I will say avoid the pull-up market as long as you can resist it because once you get a big customer, they'll come and they'll say, "I can spend a million dollars, 10 million dollars with you." And it's very tempting to want to orient your whole business around that. They will take so many of your resources. As soon as you have this enterprise customer, they will take so many of your legal, security, product, engineering, sales resources. So, you want to be really, really picky about who those first big enterprise partners are. Eventually, you're going to need, most likely, not everyone, but eventually you most likely are going to need some humans to help have these conversations. So, who do you hire? You hire someone who is AI native, who really gets AI, who lives it, who can authentically sell it because they're really bought into it. This is not a reseller right now, but this is more and more becoming the norm with sellers, with account executives. You want them to practice what they preach, you want them to be efficient, you want them to be bought into the culture of your organization. Why do you need them? I'm calling this the revenge of the steak dinner. As more and more of this becomes automated, as you have the automated outbound, the automated demo, the automated security checklist, more and more companies are craving in-person times with humans. So, conferences, conference booths work really well, dinners work really well. And they're, by the way, not that expensive to set up a dinner with some buyers, some customers, some prospects. It's a very effective tool, and you do still at this moment in time need humans to run those dinners. So, let's talk a little bit about hiring sales people and how you pay them. This comes up a lot at OpenAI. We were notorious because we still have not given out comp plans. It was not necessarily my intention to wait this long. I just kind of kept kicking the can down the road and saying, "Eventually, I'll pay commission." And then the team got bigger and it got harder and harder. I do think OpenAI is finally about to put in commission, but we made it really far without any comp plans. I would not necessarily say that is the right path for every company to follow. I think OpenAI could get away with that because of the equity packages and the way the company was increasing in value, but in general, it's really hard to hire the best enterprise sellers in the market if you don't pay a variable. That said, you can get away with it for your first few hires. Those first few sales hires are usually wired a little bit differently. They're there to build the company. They're there for the equity. They want to roll up their sleeves. They want to contribute. They're not necessarily as motivated just by commission and by a W-2. So, you can get away with it really for the first two or three sales hires. Eventually, you're going to want to hire kind of more coin-operated enterprise sellers. They're very motivated by the cash compensation, and so eventually you'll need to put in a comp plan. So, just I get this question a lot, so I just wanted to put it out there on how to think about building out comp plans. Simpler is better, but have upside for out-performers. So, I love this slide. I saw this early in my career in sales, and I just think it's a really great reminder of what you're actually selling. You're not selling your tool. You're not selling your product. You're selling what that tool or that product does to your champion or your buyer. And this is where I do think humans are still very helpful. They're very good at value selling, and I think AI does still have some ways to go on value selling. So, until we totally crack that, I do think we're going to need those steak dinners, and we're going to need those humans to sell value. All right. So, that's me. Sir Paul Marshall: Why Markets Are Getting More Competitive | Podcast | In Good Company

Norges Bank Investment Management(128.0K)·71:24·14.1K views·2026-08-26

He also discusses his philanthropic...

TL;DR

Sir Paul Marshall, co-founder of Marshall Wace, shares insights from nearly 30 years of scaling a $50 million startup into a $90 billion global hedge fund by pioneering AlphaCapture systems and fusing discretionary insight with systematic AI. He also discusses his philanthropic work with education charity ARK and his ventures into British media and politics.

Firm Origins and Evolution (1997–Present)

  • Founding Context:
    • Marshall Wace (MW) was founded in 1997 by Sir Paul Marshall and Ian Wace.
    • Started with $50 million in assets under management (AUM), including $25 million from George Soros (Stanley Druckenmiller).
    • Ranked as the third European equity long/short hedge fund at the time (alongside Odey and Egerton).
  • Capital and Regulatory Shifts:
    • Initial starting capital needed in 1997 was $50 million; today, regulations push startup costs to $300M–$500M.
    • The industry shifted from a fragmented cottage industry of fundamental stock-pickers into a concentrated, technology- and operationally-driven enterprise.
  • The 2008 Crisis and KKR Partnership:
    • AUM dropped from $14 billion to $3.5 billion in 2008 due to fund-of-funds liquidity issues and gating by competitors; Marshall Wace did not gate and served as the cash point.
    • In 2015–2016, KKR acquired a minority stake in Marshall Wace to establish a market-related valuation for the partnership and aid institutional fundraising.
  • Current Scale: Manages around $90 billion with approximately 750 employees, including over 200 in technology.

Pioneering AlphaCapture and TOPS

  • Genesis of TOPS:
    • Created in 2002 by Ian Wace and intern Anthony Clake as an internal intranet portfolio system to scientifically measure sell-side research value instead of using qualitative voting systems.
    • Initially faced skepticism from Paul Marshall, who doubted sell-side value; within months, it proved highly valuable and was monetized into a real portfolio two years later.
  • Global and Technological Rollout:
    • Expanded to the US in 2005 and Asia in 2006.
    • Evolved from alpha capture to incorporate optimization, algorithmic trading, and machine learning post-2010.

Man Plus Machine Philosophy and AI Adoption

  • Core Philosophy: Grounded in Gary Kasparov's post-Deep Blue realization that "man plus machine beats a machine."
  • AI Transformation Across the Firm:
    • Fundamental Side: AI acts as a revolutionary tool for rapid information distillation, overnight data ingestion, and augmenting fundamental portfolio managers into "authentic portfolio managers."
    • Systematic Side: Moving beyond sentiment scraping into deep context evaluation, with plans to scale from 200 quant researchers to 10,000 autonomous agentic researchers utilizing recursive self-improvement.
  • Human Superiority in Market Ecology:
    • While tasks like "nowcasting" quarterly earnings are becoming commoditized, human judgment remains vital for assessing macroeconomic ecology, systemic risk, broker/ETF leverage, and market sentiment during distress (e.g., the recent Korean market momentum crash).

Investment Philosophy, Shorting, and Debriefing

  • Fundamental Investing:
    • Combines Benjamin Graham's short-term "voting machine" (market sentiment/positioning) with the long-term "weighing machine" (free cash flow).
    • Ideal timeframe for Paul Marshall's fundamental strategy is 3 to 12 months, requiring catalyst confirmation.
  • The Difficulty of Shorting:
    • Shorting is fundamentally disadvantaged due to pro-long market ecosystems, rising costs of stock borrows, competition against elite hedge funds, and the adverse risk-management math of a stock rising against you.
  • Mistake Management and Post-Mortems:
    • Does not track drawdown limits on individual stocks; instead, focuses on portfolio-level risk management and drawdown limits.
    • Evaluates fundamental managers using top-down metrics: success/win-loss ratios, slugging ratios (conviction sizing), and alpha segmented by sector, country, long, and short.

Talent, Traits, and Hiring

  • The Shift from Degrees to Traits:
    • In the age of AI, traditional university metrics like memory and exam-passing are insufficient.
    • Key traits prioritized in hiring include agency (self-driven curiosity and entrepreneurship) and disagreeableness (the ability to challenge consensus respectfully in a civilized manner).
  • Training and Apprenticeship:
    • Runs the "Elevate" program for star graduates and an internal "chops" program allowing analysts to test stock picks before running live sub-books. Typically requires 10 years to develop a proven fund manager.

Philanthropy, Media Ventures, and Political Views

  • Education Philanthropy (ARK):
    • Co-founded ARK (Absolute Return for Kids) 26 years ago with other hedge fund managers.
    • Operates roughly 50 schools across disadvantaged areas of the UK, turning around failing schools with corporate and analytical disciplines.
  • Media Investments:
    • Founded UnHerd to challenge hegemonic, non-aligned political thinking.
    • Co-invested in GB News, which overtook BBC News and Sky as the number-one news channel in the UK by serving an unrepresented 55% of the population.
    • Acquired The Spectator (founded in 1828) to upgrade its technology and preserve its brand.
  • Brexit Perspective:
    • Supported Brexit for sovereignty, common law, and democratic accountability, but initially misjudged the economic friction of leaving the single market.
    • Criticizes the UK and Europe for regulatory overreach and failing to fully embrace innovation (such as blockchain, AI, and biotech) compared to the United States.

Notable Quotes

"Every day, one day in two, you go home feeling you're a schmuck. I can come home, I feel I'm a schmuck. I got something wrong. So you've got to be pretty head in the clouds not to understand that actually it's a very fragile path and uh and it's very difficult." — Sir Paul Marshall
"The best assurance of people doing well is their own track record and their level of experience. So I actually think the level of experience in market in markets is getting more rather than less important because of the need to be able to deal with volatility." — Sir Paul Marshall
"In the age of AI... what matters more than skills today is traits. It is not sufficient anymore that people come from a great university who got first class degrees. I don't completely trust the university system anymore... the traits you need in the age of AI include agency... and then disagreeableness... the ability to take on and challenge the consensus on any subject." — Sir Paul Marshall
