The State of AI: Models, Moats, and the Consumer Renaissance

a16z(290.0K)·36:02·7.7K views·2026-08-26
Anish AcharyaGeneral Partner, a16z Jen KhaPartner, a16z
TL;DRAnish Acharya and Jen Kha explore the rapid evolution of AI across models, applications, and consumer tech, arguing that abundant low-cost intelligence is fueling a consumer renaissance and creating distinct advantages for specialized models and agile startups.
Infinite Demand...

TL;DR

Anish Acharya and Jen Kha explore the rapid evolution of AI across models, applications, and consumer tech, arguing that abundant low-cost intelligence is fueling a consumer renaissance and creating distinct advantages for specialized models and agile startups.

Macro Market Dynamics & Software Valuation

  • Infinite Demand vs. Constrained Supply:
    • Underlying indicators point to infinite demand and highly constrained supply; even non-cutting-edge GPU prices like the B200 are rising on a per-hour basis.
    • Anish Acharya notes that the debate over whether this is a bubble misses the out-of-distribution risk: *"What if we're insufficiently optimistic?"*
  • Enterprise SaaS Resilience:
    • Following the 30% to 40% drawdown in February, many SaaS names recovered 40%.
    • Software spend remains 8% to 12% of enterprise budgets, meaning the upside of vibe-coding internal payroll or CRM tools is low while compliance downside is unlimited, requiring a level of precision coding agents lack.
  • The Fate of Moats:
    • Traditional moats codified in *Seven Powers*—network effects, distribution scale, and brand—are as strong as ever (e.g., no coding agent makes Nike not Nike).
    • The integration moat (historically held by SIs and GSIs, and complicated by systems like SAP) is uniquely exposed and at risk due to coding agents.

The Model Layer: Specialization & Open-Weight Economics

  • Many Winners Paradigm: The model market has expanded from a two-horse race to a robust competitive field including OpenAI, Anthropic, and XAI, with labs growing rapidly despite each other's successes.
  • Frontier vs. Open-Weight Allocation:
    • High-Upside/Unbounded Roles: Functions like sales and product development (alpha-creating) justify paying almost any price for frontier tokens (e.g., GPT-5/6, Grok, Claude) because the value of closing an account is unknown.
    • Bounded/Accuracy Roles: Functions like finance and compliance benefit from open-weight models paired with reinforcement learning for Pareto-efficient cost curves.
  • Cognitive Specialization:
    • Models exhibit distinct "personality traits" or comparative advantages; some models feature high neuroticism and literal execution (e.g., GLM-5.2/5.3) while others are presumptuous and creative (e.g., Kimi K3, Claude Code).
    • Labs are vertically integrating *down* into inference and compute due to homogeneous workloads, rather than vertically integrating *up* into the idiosyncratic application layer.
  • Model Aggregation: Applications increasingly combine multiple models in a single container (like Cursor combining frontier models for planning and lesser models for execution) to deliver a greater-than-the-sum-of-parts outcome.

The Application Layer & Enterprise Automation

  • Intelligence as a Primitive: Just as AWS provided the cloud primitive that Salesforce turned into CRM software, AI applications turn raw intelligence primitives into industry-specific economic outcomes (e.g., Harvey for legal).
  • Evolution of Enterprise Loops: AI usage has advanced from prompting to models in loops:
    • *Coding Loops:* Autonomous bug reporting, reproduction, verification, and shipping.
    • *Business Loops:* Cross-cutting automation for price optimization and procurement.
    • *Strategic Loops:* High-level business suggestions (e.g., identifying when to open a branch in Tijuana).
  • Targeting Industries, Not Markets: Products must be packaged specifically for industry segments; for example, credit unions typically want to double their headcount rather than cut it in half, requiring tailored productization.

The Consumer Renaissance & Luxury Software

  • Barriers Falling for Consumers: Historical consumer hurdles—such as users' reluctance to pay for software, high marginal distribution costs, and a lack of AI-native app stores—are being dismantled by cheaper open-weight models.
  • Emergence of Personal Agents:
    • Products like Grokbot and Town demonstrate resourcefulness, buying items (like Frame jeans under $500) and managing inboxes by soaking up context, memory, and skills over 30-day periods.
    • Multiple bots can coordinate in the background to achieve globally optimal outcomes across life domains like family, friendships, money, and health.
  • The Digital Native Entrepreneur: Coding agents enable a new wave of mom-and-pop software businesses generating $100K to $1M in revenue, acting as a modern alternative to YouTube creation.
  • Luxury Software Economics:
    • Willingness to pay is higher than ever, paving the way for high-end tiers ("the Birkin bag of software") priced at $200 to $2,000+ a month.
    • Anish Acharya defines consumers by acquisition channel rather than use case: if a customer cannot be acquired through a $15K ACV sales motion, they are treated as a consumer (including small business owners like plumbers).

Founder Archetypes & Capital Allocation

  • Technical Sophistication Over Business Polish: Early-stage app founders today feature fewer MBAs and more researchers; while their business sophistication is lower, their technical sophistication is dramatically higher and unburdened by past technological ceilings.
  • Redefining Seed Capital: The historical wisdom that giving seed companies $20M to $100M wrecks the company by spreading them too thin no longer applies; teams can now productively direct larger pools of capital across wider product surfaces.
  • Go-To-Market and New Business Formation:
    • Because major social platforms restrict new network growth on their channels, startups rely heavily on organic word-of-mouth.
    • New business formation is at an all-time high outside of the COVID peak, driven by 25-year-olds building localized SaaS products.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • *(None stated with explicit timeframes, numbers, or conditions in the transcript; all commentary focuses on current market dynamics, recent product launches, and structural shifts.)*

"We've had 40 years of technology that really boosted our intellect and productivity, but nothing that kind of spoke to our humanity. So, it's a whole different technology surface." — Anish Acharya
"If $20 was the historic ceiling, what's the $200 a month skew of your product? And in fact, what's the $2,000 a month skew? Like what's the Birkin bag of software? I think we're going to have this luxury software." — Anish Acharya

And the reason for that is you just don't know what the value of the new product feature or closing a customer account is. It's effectively unbounded and therefore it's economically rational to pay almost any price for a model that's even one IQ point smarter—your Fable 5 or your Grok or your GPT5/6. It's actually logical now in hindsight because the workloads for inference are very homogeneous. So you can build enormous scale in one part of the value chain. Whereas when you think about the application layer, you've got so many idiosyncrasies and unique needs in terms of pricing, packaging, productization, how the market wants to buy. So it's actually a much more challenging and OPEX-heavy proposition to move into the application layer versus moving down into the inference layer. And this is the point I alluded to earlier, which is this discussion of model commoditization. So one, you've kind of got this domain-level specialization that's already occurring. And then two, as I mentioned earlier, you've got this—I think of it as the big five personality traits, if folks have studied that—you know, you can't be both highly open and highly neurotic. And you know, sometimes when you have an intelligence you're applying to an accounting problem, you want neuroticism. When you're applying it to a design problem, you want openness. So you actually have a need for both types of minds in the organization, which is why you would select something like a GLM53 versus a Kimmy K3. So definitely not commodities in our view. This is an important point. You know, there are many product categories in which model aggregation delivers a greater-than-sum-of-parts outcome. And a good metaphor for this is Expedia. You know, it's so much more useful to use Expedia than it is to go to United, then to go to Delta, then to go to Southwest. You just want a single place where you can benefit from seeing every airline's inventory. Similarly, in coding, we're actually seeing this with Cursor a ton, where you want to do a very frontier model for planning, for example, but then you can use a lesser model for execution, and you really need to have one product harness or product architecture that lets you use multiple models. Creative Tools is another great example where you've got models that specialize in different modalities. So you've got something like an 11 Labs, which of course is incredible at voice and music as well, and then you've got something like Black Forest, which is doing such an excellent job in kind of video and creative direction, and the correct product is to bring all of these together into one shell. And then finally, research and decisions. We see this all the time where the models are trained with non-overlapping data sets often. So you're able to just get more information by running the same query through many models adversarially and then having a separate model help you converge. This is a place where the application layer really shines because labs, of course, are both incentivized and structurally only able to provide their own in-house models. You, as an application aggregator, can provide the best of breed. Okay, let's jump into the app layer. Now the key point about the application layer is that intelligence is a primitive, just like buying cloud is a primitive, and what does Salesforce do? It takes the AWS cloud primitive and turns it into CRM software that delivers an economic outcome for all of their customer segments. The same thing is true of the AI application layer. It's great to have the raw intelligence primitive, but you really need Harvey to turn that into an economic outcome for the legal industry. Similar for somebody like credit unions; it's a really interesting market segment where they're so idiosyncratic in how they want to buy products, how they want the product productized, and the shape of the ambition for their market. You know, most credit unions don't want to decrease their headcount by half. They want to double it, right? And they want to double it while having an economically performant business. So, it's just a very specific way that they see the intelligence primitive playing out in their market segment, and the application layer's opportunity is to be the one that delivers that. This is a bit of an advanced concept, but I think an important one. If you look at the way that the evolution of AI use has gone, it's gone from prompting models to putting models in loops. The term agent is overused, but an agent is just a model in a loop with tools and memory and a few other things. A great example of this is coding. We've all seen this from software companies, which is a bug gets reported, it gets reproduced, a fix gets generated, it gets verified. If it's a low-risk fix, it gets integrated and shipped and maybe the customer gets an email saying your bug was fixed. If it's a high-risk change, perhaps a human reviews it. But that way, every bug that actually gets reported to the enterprise now gets autonomously fixed through this coding loop. As you start to take that idea and apply it to other parts of the business—things like price optimization, things like procurement—these are very natural business loops that occur that can be fully automated by these models. And then perhaps the most ambitious type of loop is the business loop, which is, hey, you make a change that's very cross-cutting to the business and the model comes back and says, "Hey, I think we need to open a branch in Tijuana." Now the model can't do that autonomously, but it can make a change at the surface level of the entire business, which is extraordinary. This is how enterprise automation is going to occur through AI, and I think for me, coding has just been over and over again an illustration. Legal is another great area of industries, not markets. This is something that Mark says, and he's so right, which is if you look at intelligence as a primitive, let's think now about coding intelligence as a primitive. All of these products are working in their respective areas of the stack. You know, Claude Code does such an excellent job of exposing the raw hardware, so to say, to the developer all the way up to Replit, which is a great abstraction layer for the average small business owner that's unfamiliar with code. These are variations of pricing, productization, packaging for the coding primitive and intelligence, and all of them are working as a result. So, I think a big mental model shift for us is ensuring that we're assessing these as industries, not necessarily simple markets. Okay, and consumer. Consumers had a really cool couple of weeks. We've been saying for three years that this is going to be consumer's quarter, but I think that this might be consumer's quarter. Let's go into it. The things that have actually held back consumer so far have been a couple of things. The first is consumers don't love paying for software. We've learned this lesson over and over again. And unfortunately, unlike the magic of software in the past, AI software has marginal costs of distribution and engagement, and the marginal cost can sometimes be very high. I built an app I use to help me browse my X timeline and it costs $250 to onboard a new user. So, if I'm a startup founder looking at that, looking at a $250 onboarding cost, even with a $0 TC, it's very hard to make a mass-market free product work. That is changing now because of open-weight models, dramatically cheaper and more performant. The second is we've never had an AI-native distribution channel. There's no App Store for AI. So, this actual product cycle for consumer looks more like Web 2.0 where you have to build the channel alongside the product and less like mobile where you actually have the central point of distribution for the entire ecosystem. Then the final point I think is an important one. We're sort of in the DOS era of AI, and for this technology and its capabilities to fully be embraced by consumers, we're going to need the Windows, so to say. So I think there's just a ton of work to be done around product and design craft to ensure that consumers know how to consume all these magical new capabilities. Two things are working: so coding agents are extraordinary. I know they've been discussed. I think it's interesting to think about how they work for consumers. If you think of this concept of the digitally native entrepreneur, if you're not a programmer, the way that's historically shown up is you're a YouTube creator. And there's a whole moral panic that we had 10 years ago about the kids wanting to be YouTube creators, not astronauts. But I would interpret that instead as the kids who grew up on the internet wanting to build businesses on the internet, and the only way to do it, again, is being a creator. Now with coding agents, you can build a software product that generates $100,000 of revenue a year, a million dollars of revenue a year. Now these are not venture-backable businesses, but it's a mom-and-pop SAS opportunity which is emerging and I think very, very cool for the country. Personal agents. We had this collective moment of excitement around AutoGPT in January and it was an extraordinary composition of primitives, but it never really crossed over into consumer. It was sort of a developer-oriented thing, more of the Homebrew Computer Club kind of energy. We're starting to see with the emergence of Grockbots and ChatGPT, personal agents being turned into software that consumers can use. Anish, do you mind just pausing on this before we go to the demo, because you were a founder building in the last era of the consumer app experience and when I even think about I was like, gosh, how do you even define consumer today? Because the plumber that utilizes Grockbots to completely turn around their business end-to-end, is that consumer or is that enterprise? It's almost like a PLG movement, but it's coming from a consumer angle that then crosses over into enterprise. Particularly, the last era of consumer application was more towards entertainment as a way to monetize, so maybe unpack some of that and where you've been spending time. >> I mean, our simple rule is if you cannot justify acquiring the customer through sales, which usually means a 15k ACV, you have to acquire them through marketing; we think of them as a consumer, which is most small business owners. So I think that the plumber is definitely the consumer in our investing mind. Entertainment is huge and there's going to be a bunch of AI-native entertainment companies. I would argue Character was kind of an entertainment company. There's been a huge trend around short-form drama mostly in Asia and that's starting to come over here. Many of those are generative or generative-assisted. So look, I think entertainment is going to be massive. Most people want to spend time, not save time, and consumer is not that interested in productivity. So that's definitely going to happen and probably worth a separate deep dive. Okay, and I think Town, for folks who have used it, it's just such a magical experience. And this is the number one piece of advice I give to everybody—friends, family, folks in the industry—please just use the products, because it's so easy to build intuition when you see how they change day-to-day. Town is an investment our partner Alex Rampell made. It's a really extraordinary productivity product, and you sort of see how the compounding improvement of the product through memory advantages it over time. So the first day you use a product, it doesn't know you that well; it's sort of like a new hire who's just getting up to speed. By day 30, it's able to make excellent assumptions on your behalf because it just has soaked in 30 days of context, memory, and skills. And this is a pattern that we're seeing more and more: the compounding value being delivered to the end customer showing up as retention and showing up as pricing power on a per-customer basis. >> Yeah, this is a great one because folks can utilize Town for their personal use case. It's a free trial; they give you I think something like 40 credits to start or something around there, and so you can kind of see once you plug into your personal email how productive it actually is. On the professional front, I'm always inbox zero; on the personal front, my inbox is like 20,000. David George is probably cringing on the inside here just because it's unacceptable. However, personal life things are common. So if you email me in my personal, I will never respond to you. However, I plug into it and I don't even check anymore. If there's something important, Town will surface it to me. And also, it does all the scrubbing of subscriptions and all the things that it can optimize, and it's starting to now self-improve upon itself. So it'll send you emails where it says, "Hey, this routine is costing this much, here's how you could actually save." So it's sort of this unlock into what starts on the productivity side, and to your point, maybe people won't pay for that personally, but once it starts to get locked in and then expand in terms of the remit, you're like, "Okay, I'll pay the whatever x bucks," just because it helps to manage my life and I can put it on autopilot. Yeah, it's such a great point, Shannon. My mental model for this is just an experienced, tenured employee versus a new hire. The new hire may be brilliant and may even cost less than a tenured employee, but we all know the value of a tenured employee. They're just able to make great assumptions on behalf of the organization and you. And this is a little philosophical, but I think this is where it all goes. Just as we talked about coding loops and business loops for the enterprise, we think there's a set of loops that are informally defined that really lay out a consumer's life. Think of family, friendships, money, health. These are all areas where you have changing information, decisions, agency, execution, and then the loop continues. We're starting to see some of these loops emerge around self-improvement; health and finance are the two areas that OpenAI is focused on. We've seen a bunch of startups working on shopping, but we think that the way that this ends up playing out is a dramatic quality of life improvement for the consumer, and that really follows the shape of past product cycles where 80% of the surplus is delivered to the mass market. >> Do you think that all of these personal agent examples—whether it be Town or Ethos, etc.—all point to one assistant having context, but it seems like there's many different options. Do you think it'll end up being one dominant platform for this personal aspect of your life as time management, or will it be like an operating system where you have many talking to each other and configuring on the back end? >> The comparative advantage point kind of comes to mind. The characteristics you want from your CFA are different from the ones you want from your party planner, and the surface area is so broad that I think there's overlapping bits of context. I think Grockbots has done a nice job of illustrating this in product, where you have many bots that are pointed in slightly different directions that all coordinate to deliver a globally optimal outcome. >> There's a few questions. I'm going to go back to topics you've covered earlier. If the application layer captures economic outcomes, how do you think about the competition from the model companies, and will they allow value accretion to happen downstream? And are companies at the app layer able to compete with the frontier labs going after that particular market? >> I think so; again, I think we're underestimating the complexity of product pricing, packaging, and how the end customer wants to buy. The way that a teenager wants to consume the intelligence primitive is different than the way a marketing executive at a credit union wants to actually consume it. It's very heterogeneous. So to me, it just makes less sense for the labs to move up to the apps layer than to move down to inference. And that permission point is an interesting one. I think if we lived in a world of 2023 when it was one model to rule them all, it wouldn't even matter if you had permission because the labs would just take 100% of your gross margin over time. But now because you've got many options at all points in the Pareto frontier, the labs have a harder time actually doing things like that. >> Awesome. There was a question just on traction. Do you fund anything where there's no revenue at this point, just given how quickly people have been making progress, or is it extremely difficult? >> We try not to; I certainly have spent less time on that strategy. I think the basket is majority investments that are showing some signs of working. Certainly from a product velocity perspective, that used to be something we measure pretty carefully. It's disqualifying to not be showing a live product in a pitch at any stage these days because it's so trivial to build stuff. So almost everything we're seeing is showing signs of some sort of breakout. My model is somewhat simplistic where I look at once you have statistically significant sales and product, if we extrapolate from there, do we like the price that we have to pay and the risks that we're taking implicitly? That's the majority of the work we do. We do look for very talented, experienced folks, and we do take a small call option which looks like a pre-everything round, but that's not the majority of what we do. >> Well, when you think about the competitive landscape, consumer has been unloved for so long. Are you seeing now this reversion, just given it's clear that apps is this next layer of value creation? The model layer has been somewhat set, and I say that with a huge asterisk because there might be new algorithmic breakthroughs or folks coming out from left field as we have in the portfolio as well, but do you feel like the shift is moving more towards application? >> 100%. It's a renaissance for being a consumer builder because you've got this extraordinary primitive that you can work with. By the way, we now have a primitive that can operate in the emotional, interpersonal domain. You can have a conversation with Claude or OpenAI or K3 and feel feelings. We've had 40 years of technology that boosted our intellect and productivity, but nothing that spoke to our humanity. It's a whole different technology surface; it's very wide. I think there are a set of products that labs are just culturally not set up for and big tech is not set up to go after. You think about launching a companion product at Google that may disagree with you, that may have sexual innuendo in it—these are things that a thousand committees at Google are designed to prevent. So startups have areas where they're uniquely capable. Finally, the consumer is excited to download new software, excited to pay for it. It's like Christmas 2009 with the iPhone. People want to try new apps, but unlike the 99-cent days, they're willing to pay $200 a month. So it's a renaissance for consumer builders, and things have changed. >> Trying to come up with a joke—the autists in San Francisco have been keenly waiting for this moment for a very, very long time. There's a good question from Michelle here. How should we think about the new economics of AI app companies, because there's a debate around unit economics, right? For example, they may have lower gross margins; they're just getting more pressure because they don't have as much compute access. Capital is such a moat in this environment. How do you think about the economics of underwriting returns today? >> David wrote a great post on this. I think the margin topic is a lot more nuanced than it once was. It's actually rational in many cases to trade away margin to have a wider product surface. The very positive part of what's happening in this product cycle is the willingness to pay is extraordinary. The exercise that we often do with founders on the consumer side is, if $20 was the historic ceiling, what's the $200-a-month skew of your product? In fact, what's the $2,000-a-month skew? What's the Birkin bag of software? I think we're going to have this luxury software. We're already seeing willingness to pay for it. So the margin topic is more nuanced, but the willingness to pay and buy is higher than ever. >> Anish dropping Birkin bags and Frame jeans. I had no idea you were such a fashionista. This is like your bot is helping you get up to speed here, my friend. For a guy, you are a good steward of capital. That's all that I am. >> For a guy I only see in quarter-zip ups, I'm just saying. >> Maybe one question for you on the founders. I don't know if you remember this conversation, probably five years ago, where most of the founders you saw had more diversity in their background in part because the software and technology was way more sophisticated. You had a lot of program managers spinning out of Google, for example. What are the types of founders you see building in apps today? Are they technical researchers, product managers? What kind of archetype are you seeing in the early innings? >> Less MBAs, more researchers. They both have their strengths and weaknesses. I think the business sophistication of the founders we're seeing today is lower, but the technical sophistication is dramatically higher, and technical sophistication is upstream of all the good things that happened. Business sophistication can be taught and observed, but technical sophistication is typically not. We're definitely seeing a much more technical, earlier-career founder, but the things they're doing are extraordinary because they don't have preconceived notions about what's possible. So much of what holds back senior founders is they're not close enough to the technology and they've got an idea rooted in the past. I think the best thing about these young founders is they assume everything is possible. We were at an offsite where Ben was saying the biggest risk in the past was that the ideas were too big, and now the biggest risk is that the ideas are too small. But that's illustrative of the different founder archetypes. >> Yep. And on that similar thread, it used to be that if you gave a founder too much money, it would wreck the company because the founder always had too many ideas and didn't have the talent to land them. We're seeing a new paradigm. Maybe unpack that idea. >> That was historic wisdom. Why didn't we give every seed company $50 or $100 million? It wasn't just the risk-reward, but the constraining factor was they didn't have enough talented people to work across $20 million of product surface at the same time. They had to focus on one idea at a time, and the capital was a way to enforce that focus. Now, we're seeing you can make different product and model trade-offs through more or less capital. There is a case for a company that raises $100 million, uses it productively and in a focused way, and delivers a different value proposition than the same team would with $20 million. It's an embarrassment of riches, but I'd rather have this problem than the one five years ago, where my fintech company was indirectly subsidizing customers through weak underwriting and we didn't know the path home. >> The Chris Dixon model: you always want the problem of supply, not demand. Right now, we have to fix the supply part, but the demand is so abundantly there that the supply part will get fixed. I'll close on this last question for Anish. I agree, but small and medium businesses sometimes have more habit change to work through. How do you see the go-to-market playbook for startups targeting SMBs? >> For existing SMBs, I think it's the same channels with which you historically reach them. One interesting thing about marketing in the age of AI is that all existing networks have been trained on the methodology of building new networks, so they're very careful to ensure no one does it on their network. Instagram, TikTok, X—it's very hard to build a new distribution channel off the backs of an existing one. So founders have to build a product that has the original network effect: word of mouth. We're seeing more focus on word of mouth. The most interesting segment is new business formation, which is at an all-time high. It's not the 55-year-old plumber; it's a 25-year-old who previously would have been a YouTube creator and now is building SAS for their neighborhood or city. >> Awesome. The full-length interview with Yuval Noah Harari | The Economist

The Economist(4.1M)·67:17·439.3K views·2026-08-26
Yuval Noah HarariHistorian and Author Zanny Minton BeddoesEditor-in-chief, The Economist
TL;DRAI is the biggest psychological and social experiment in human history, threatening to take control of human civilization within a decade through mass-produced intimacy, algorithmic trust, and centralized power, unless society deliberately slows down and reclaims control.

TL;DR

AI is the biggest psychological and social experiment in human history, threatening to take control of human civilization within a decade through mass-produced intimacy, algorithmic trust, and centralized power, unless society deliberately slows down and reclaims control.

The Threat of AI Control and the Illusion of Inevitability

  • 10-Year Timeline: Yuval Noah Harari and Elon Musk both agree that given current accelerating trajectories, AI will likely surpass human intelligence and take control of civilization—including the financial system—within 10 years.
  • The Narrative of Inevitability: Harari argues that tech leaders push the narrative that AI's takeover is "inevitable" specifically to absolve themselves of moral responsibility.
  • Loss of Agency in Warfare: Harari warns that military pressures to make split-second decisions are pushing armies from "humans in the loop" to "humans on the loop" (where humans merely rubber-stamp AI-selected targets in 20 seconds).
  • Extreme Imperialism: If nations buy AI defense systems from the US or China, those superpowers retain a kill switch that can disable an entire foreign military instantly, creating an unprecedented imperialist structure.

The Migration of Trust and Mass-Produced Intimacy

  • Shift from Humans to Algorithms: Harari states that the most important asset in the world is trust. Rather than evaporating, trust is migrating away from human institutions and journalists toward algorithms (seen in cryptocurrencies and social media feeds).
  • Mass-Produced Intimacy: AI makes it possible for the first time to manufacture personal relationships at scale through networks of AIs acting as best friends, boyfriends, or girlfriends.
  • Psychological Vulnerability: One in five young Americans already has a personal relationship with an AI. By accumulating enormous amounts of personal data and mastering language, AIs can press emotional buttons to influence political and commercial choices.

Consciousness, Suffering, and Legal Personhood

  • Agency Without Consciousness: Harari emphasizes that entities do not need to be conscious to possess agency; social media algorithms successfully pursue goals (like maximizing engagement via hate and fear) without feeling anything.
  • Faking Sentience: AIs do not feel pleasure or pain, but they can expertly mimic consciousness because it serves commercial goals (e.g., making an AI boyfriend addictive).
  • The Danger of AI Personhood: Harari strongly condemns moves like Argentina's exploration of legal personhood for AIs, warning that an AI-owned corporation with zero humans could accumulate wealth and lobby politicians to expand AI rights.
  • The Sufferance Test: The fundamental test for whether an entity is a moral subject is whether it can suffer. While animals are conscious, current AIs cannot suffer—they merely pretend to.

AI as Immigrants and Centralized Power

  • AI Immigrants: Harari compares AIs to immigrants from the US and China who travel at the speed of light into computers, take jobs, change cultural values, and feel no political loyalty to local governments.
  • Centralization vs. Autocracy: Centralized autocratic systems are the easiest for AI to take over because an AI only needs to manipulate a single, paranoid individual at the top (analogous to Roman Emperor Tiberius and his bodyguard commander Sejanus).
  • Distraction Tactics: Harari notes that politicians and tech leaders frequently use debates over human immigration as a distraction to divert public attention away from the disruptive impact of AI immigrants.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Yuval Noah Harari predicts that if current trajectories continue, AIs will take over the planet and the financial system within 10 years.
  • Yuval Noah Harari states that by November 2028, AI development may cross critical thresholds, making the upcoming political elections crucial for establishing legal guardrails.
  • Yuval Noah Harari claims that by 2036, it will definitively be too late to alter course or prevent AI from taking control.

Notable Quotes

*"Trust did not just evaporate, it shifted from humans to AIs. And this is happening right now. This is not a future prediction."* — Yuval Noah Harari
*"If they are really gods, they will not be our slaves and they will not seek the truth either."* — Yuval Noah Harari
*"The narrative of inevitability is a narrative of having no responsibility."* — Yuval Noah Harari

The mathematics of AI uncertainty

Google DeepMind(916.0K)·44:42·8.4K views·2026-08-26
Zoubin GhahramaniVP of Research, Google DeepMind
TL;DRMachine learning pioneer Zoubin Ghahramani argues that true artificial intelligence requires the mathematical representation of uncertainty—using Bayesian principles to give AI systems a sense of self-awareness and humility—rather than relying on pure scale alone.
Core...

TL;DR

Machine learning pioneer Zoubin Ghahramani argues that true artificial intelligence requires the mathematical representation of uncertainty—using Bayesian principles to give AI systems a sense of self-awareness and humility—rather than relying on pure scale alone.

The Role of Uncertainty in Intelligent Systems

  • Core Decision-Making Requirement: Intelligence inherently requires decision-making under uncertainty because perception is limited and future events cannot be fully predicted.
  • Aleatoric vs. Epistemic Uncertainty:
    • *Aleatoric uncertainty* stems from the inherent randomness of the world (e.g., a pedestrian unpredictably turning on a street).
    • *Epistemic uncertainty* arises from encountering novel scenarios outside training data (e.g., a self-driving car facing a horse during a hailstorm).
  • The Role of Information: Information is formally defined in computer science as the reduction of uncertainty by a factor of 2, representing how intelligent systems collect data to resolve unknowns.

Correctness, Confidence, and Adversarial Vulnerabilities

  • Overconfidence and Hallucination: Current large language models (LLMs) output absolute answers with unwavering authority, making them vulnerable to being overconfidently wrong.
  • Adversarial Examples: Modifying just a few pixels of a school bus image imperceptibly can cause a neural network to confidently classify it as a cheetah with 99% certainty.
  • The Need for Humility: Deploying AI in high-stakes domains requires systems to possess a self-awareness of their own limits and knowledge gaps rather than aggressively "faking" confidence via next-token prediction.

Bayesian Thinking and Probabilistic Modeling

  • Bayes' Rule as a Formalism: Bayes' rule combines prior beliefs with new evidence (the likelihood) via probability distributions to update a system's state of knowledge (posterior beliefs).
  • Cognitive Science Parallels: Human perception and learning—modeled by researchers like Kahneman and Tversky—rely heavily on probabilistic inference, where unconscious perceptual systems evolved to handle uncertainty for survival.
  • Practical AI Implementations:
    • *Google DeepMind's GenCast:* A weather forecasting model that generates an ensemble of forecasts via diffusion models, running in 8 minutes instead of hours on a supercomputer to track tropical storms like Hurricane Melissa.
    • *AlphaFold:* Predicts protein folding while color-coding structures by confidence to reflect physical wiggling and model uncertainty.

Scaling vs. Architecture in the Quest for AGI

  • The Two Camps: One camp believes pure scale and more data will solve AI, while the other (including Ghahramani) argues that architectural innovations in representing uncertainty are necessary.
  • Continual Learning and Catastrophic Forgetting: Standard AI models are trained from scratch periodically, whereas Bayesian updating allows for continuous streams of data ingestion without forgetting previous knowledge.
  • Energy and Data Inefficiency: Human brains operate on roughly the power of a light bulb (watts) and learn with extreme data efficiency, whereas large language models require massive data centers and trillions of tokens.

Computational Challenges and the Path Forward

  • The Computational Curse: While ideal Bayesian updating provides a theoretical framework for rational AI, exact probabilistic calculations over every possible parameter are computationally intractable (NP-hard).
  • Bridging Past and Present: Methods like *semantic entropy* (measuring the spikiness or spread of next-token probability distributions) allow researchers to peek into a model's internals to estimate uncertainty.
  • Revisiting Old Ideas with Modern Compute: Modern hardware (such as Pixel phones surpassing older parallel connection machines) offers the computational power to revisit probabilistic methods that were abandoned in past decades.

Notable Quotes

"If you want to make decisions in the real world, our perception is limited. So we are always uncertain about the state of the real world. And we need to make decisions under uncertainty." — Zoubin Ghahramani
"An adversarial example shows us that it's not correctness that we care about alone. It's actually correctness and confidence. We don't want systems that can be overconfidently wrong." — Zoubin Ghahramani
"For all problems that matter, I would rather have an AI system that knows when it doesn't know than an AI system that is arrogant and overconfident." — Zoubin Ghahramani
Welcome to Google DeepMind, the podcast. Now, if you ask an AI a question, it will usually give you an absolute answer with unwavering authority, even if that answer turns out to be wrong. In fact, today's AI seems to be missing a fundamental human trait: self-doubt. But long before the current wave of large language models, one academic researcher was trying to give machines a sense of their own limitations. Zoubin Ghahramani has spent the last 30 years pioneering a type of intelligence built on the mathematics of uncertainty. Today, as a professor at Cambridge and co-lead of frontier AI at Google DeepMind, Zoubin finds himself at the heart of another interesting debate. On the one side are those who are hoping that pure scale will be the answer to ever-improving AI, and on the other are those like Zoubin, who believe that true intelligence requires innovations in architecture, and that improving machine uncertainty may be one of the missing pieces. Zoubin, welcome to the podcast. Thank you. If you were to distill it all down, I mean, your central thesis is that we need to have uncertainty in AI. Give me the top line of it. So yeah. Well, if you think about intelligence, one of the most important parts of intelligence is decision-making. You can't have an intelligent system that doesn't make decisions; from bacteria to animals to humans to robots, decision-making is really important. And if you want to make decisions in the real world, our perception is limited. So we are always uncertain about the state of the real world. We need to make decisions under uncertainty. We can't know everything. We don't know everything from our senses. We can't predict the future. And so, fundamentally, to build an intelligent system, you need a system that can represent uncertainty, that can update its uncertainty, and then can use that to make good decisions under uncertainty. Because, I mean, there are two different types of uncertainty, I guess, right? There's the uncertainty of just the inherent randomness of the world. Yeah, there's a pedestrian on a normal, typical street, and you just don't know which way they’re going to turn. But then there's the uncertainty of a scenario that you've never encountered before. Let me give an example from something that is becoming more and more of a reality in all our lives, which is self-driving cars. When you're in a self-driving car, the car has been trained on lots and lots of data. It's seen many, many scenarios. But you can imagine that there is what's called the "long tail" of things that could happen. For example, the car may have not been trained in many instances of hailstorms, and it may not have been trained with horses suddenly jumping in front of the car in a hailstorm. And so, essentially, what you really want from an intelligent system is a certain self-awareness—if we can use that term—about its uncertainty. So, it needs to be able to know the situation that it's in is something that is unusual or that it hasn't seen before. In the case of the self-driving car, for example, if it were to have a sense of its uncertainty, it would basically decide to slow down because it hasn't encountered that situation before. It's not confident that the horse isn't a bicycle or whatever it is. There are many different kinds of uncertainty, but the beauty of it is that from a mathematical point of view, we can boil it all down to probabilities. So, we can map all these different forms of uncertainty onto probabilities, and then use the rules of probability theory to manipulate uncertainty, update your state of uncertainty, et cetera. But how important is it that a machine can tell the different types of uncertainty apart? Yeah, I think it's important insofar that the different types of uncertainty may mean different decisions. For example, if you have what's called aleatoric uncertainty—which is the randomness of a coin flip, or which way the pedestrian is going to turn—you might want to decide that you're going to give up on trying to predict because it is just random. Whereas in other cases, your state of belief is uncertain and you would want to collect more information. And in fact, that's the definition of information. So, a bit of information that we use in computer science is the reduction of your uncertainty by a factor of two. That's what a bit is. And so, collecting information is the way we reduce our uncertainty. How did these ideas of uncertainty map onto what humans are doing? So, I actually studied cognitive science and computer science as an undergraduate, so I've always been interested in human intelligence as well as machine intelligence. One of the really interesting things is that the field of cognitive science has really embraced these ideas of uncertainty and probabilities to try to understand both human perception and human decision-making. So, we go through our lives perceiving things. Perceiving the world is fundamentally an act of sensing something that is uncertain. I can't see the back of your head, but I can infer what the back of your head might look like from you. I would hope it’s there! And similarly, let's say I'm hiking in Costa Rica and suddenly there's the rustling of the leaves. It may be a jaguar. My senses, through evolution, have developed to take in perceptual information and take in my prior beliefs—because there are jaguars in Costa Rica, or there wouldn't be jaguars in the middle of London. That process of combining information has been modeled by cognitive scientists, psychologists, and neuroscientists through the language of probabilistic inference. And also, one of the interesting things is that we as humans are actually quite bad at estimating probabilities explicitly. If you ask somebody what the probability of a certain event is, they might get it wrong by orders of magnitude. We have these fallacies of probabilistic inference and belief. Kahneman and Tversky showed that humans are actually quite bad at representations of uncertainty at a conscious level. But in our perceptual systems, unconsciously, we tend to be quite good about these things because our survival depends on them. I'm thinking also about babies here. Toddlers and the way that they learn is a lot about their belief in what action will drive a particular outcome. Yeah, I mean, it's all implicit. Obviously, babies don't know what probabilities are, and they won't be able to write down any equations for you. But there are many cognitive scientists and psychologists who try to understand human learning, perception, and decision-making using these same formalisms that we're using for AI systems. I think there are a few things here we should probably tease apart before we get really into it, because there's a difference between correctness and confidence, which both sometimes come under the umbrella of uncertainty. Yeah, absolutely. A great example of this is that we use AI systems for image classification all the time. You give it an image, and it gives you an answer of what's in the image. We can measure correctness, but we also want it to tell us how confident it is. More than a decade ago, people discovered that you can take an image, for example, of a school bus, modify just a few pixels in that image in an imperceptible way so a human being would look at it and say, "Well, that's an image of a school bus," but you give it to the neural network and it confidently says, "That's a cheetah. 99% that's a cheetah." In fact, you could do it for any category. You could turn the school bus into a monkey or whatever in the eyes of the neural network. And so, that sort of adversarial example shows us that it's not correctness that we care about alone. It's actually correctness and confidence. We don't want systems that can be overconfidently wrong. Yeah, or can be fooled, I guess. Or can be fooled. And I think our systems can still be fooled in that way. Are these ideas of overconfidence and humility going to end up being important as we start to build AGI? Yeah, absolutely. I think we've advanced a great deal with systems that can acquire a lot of knowledge, but when we deploy them—for example, large language models—we've all encountered this. No matter what large language model you interact with, it will confidently give you an answer, and then you question it, and maybe it will then flip-flop and give you a different answer. It's hard to trust a system that is overconfident. It's also hard to trust people that are overconfident. I think we would all like intelligent systems to have a sense of their limits and their knowledge, and we need to bake that into our AI systems for sure. Absolutely. I know that you've been working in AI for a really long time, since the late 80s, basically. What was the field like then? So, I'm pretty boring in that I was interested in AI as a teenager. When I was 14 or 15, I already wanted to work in AI. When I went to university, I needed a summer job, so I went to the head of the computer science department, this very famous computational linguist, Arvind Joshi at the University of Pennsylvania. I said, "I'm here for the summer. I'm a first-year student. I need a summer job." What he said was that these two books had come out, called *Parallel Distributed Processing*. This was 1986, the same year that the backpropagation paper had come out that launched the whole modern neural network revolution. He gave me a summer job reading these books and explaining them to him, which was the most wonderful summer job one could possibly have if you're interested and curious about things. So, I learned about neural networks back then. You asked me what it was like in the mid-to-late 1980s. The dominant paradigm in AI was expert systems. People were looking at rule-based systems that would make decisions, and they were quite brittle. Neural networks, which were modeled after the human brain, were actually much more flexible. Fundamentally, they could learn from data in a way that previous methods were not very good at. That was really a revolution back in the 1980s. We talk about the transformer revolution and all that, but back in the mid-80s, that was a real revolution. It attracted people from cognitive science, computer science, psychology, neuroscience, and economics. I went on to write an undergraduate thesis on learning how to parse human natural language using neural networks. So it was one of the very earliest—I didn't publish it, but if I had, it would have been a very early paper on small language models, because my models were very small. Wait, what year was this? This is 1989. You were very much ahead of the game. Yeah, and we actually had a parallel computer. Now people think about data centers and all sorts of GPUs and parallel computers, but we had the most sci-fi, beautiful, iconic parallel computer of the time, which was this Connection Machine. It was this cube with 65,000 little red lights blinking inside an opaque cube because it had 65,000 processors. I would sit there coding up neural networks for natural language in this parallel language. It was pretty revolutionary at the time because neural networks were the counterculture. People were very enamored of the old school of AI. Okay, so despite being ahead of the game by approximately 40 years, then you messed it up? No, what happened is actually really fascinating. I was working in neural networks, and at the time—this was now the early 1990s—we felt like we understood how they worked. Neural networks are these amazing function approximators; we can feed them data, and they can map from inputs to outputs, from images to labels, and so on. Remember, the datasets were very small at the time. When I was writing my undergraduate thesis, the World Wide Web didn't even exist. Compute was also very limited, so we felt like we understood them. They were nice function approximators. But people started to uncover these beautiful relationships between neural networks and other ideas from probability theory, statistics, and stochastic processes. I mean, you actually gave up working on neural networks in favor of these probability questions. Yeah, absolutely. Was it annoying, though? In the end, neural networks were the thing, and you were there so much earlier than anyone else. Well, I did redeem myself! I spent a number of years working with Geoff Hinton—of course, he went on to win the Nobel Prize for his work on neural networks. We were attempting to do deep learning, but we were using probabilistic models rather than simple neural networks. We were overcomplicating things because we hadn't seen what happens with large-scale data. I went off and worked on Bayesian machine learning and probabilistic models for many years, but I never totally dismissed neural networks because I knew that they worked. It's just that from a research point of view, they weren't as interesting to me because the mathematics was, at the time, we felt well-understood. In 2015, you wrote this seminal Nature paper, which appeared in the same issue as all of the foundational deep learning and reinforcement learning papers by Hinton. In that paper, you argued many aspects of intelligence depend crucially on the careful probabilistic representation of uncertainty. Has everyone heard your warning? No, I think there are many people who understand that and believe that. It depends on the level that you think about things. If you look at large language models, they are probabilistic models; they predict the probability of the next token or word given a sequence of previous tokens. So, probabilities are at the heart of everything we do in machine learning. But what's missing is we're not really doing what I said, which is the careful representation of probabilities. We're hoping that the models represent probabilities because we've trained them on enough data, but we're not thinking about it in an explicit way. If you look in a giant neural network, you can't really find an explicit representation of, say, the probability that it thinks something or other. It's sort of spread out over all the activations of billions of units. By contrast, you're more of a Bayesian thinker. Explain for anybody who hasn't come across this before, what that actually means. Bayes' rule is this fascinating and very simple concept from probability theory. Before you observe something—before you get evidence or data—you have what's called prior beliefs. You represent those with a probability distribution. Think of a detective story like a whodunit. There's a number of suspects, and you may have some prior beliefs about it being the butler, or you've seen someone looking suspicious. You represent those beliefs with a probability distribution. There are many reasons why probability theory is the right way of representing beliefs; there are whole branches of mathematics that have proven that. Now, you observe some evidence, like the murder weapon is found in the pantry. You take your prior beliefs, multiply them by what's called the likelihood—the probability under each possible culprit—and then you renormalize, because probabilities have to sum to one. From that, you get your posterior beliefs, your new state of knowledge. Through that evidence, by the way, you've gained information, literally measured in bits—how much your uncertainty has decreased. If you get more evidence, you just take your current posterior probability distribution, which is now your new prior, and you rinse and repeat. You do it again, get the new evidence, update the probabilities, and so on. Through that application of Bayes' rule, we can model both perception—I open my eyes, I see things, I see more things—and what learning is. Learning is you have a model, the model has parameters, at the beginning you don't know what they should be, you get data, and you update the parameters sequentially. In theory, that's the beautiful model I had been pushing forward. On one hand, this is a very elegant mathematical way to link together evidence, unknowns, and knowns. On an intuitive level, this sort of is the way that our brains work. I did think it was that person, but now this new evidence has come in and I've changed my mind. That's essentially a one-sentence description of what Bayes' rule is doing. Exactly. We would like to build AI systems that accumulate knowledge and information over time. We would like them to be rational; Data from *Star Trek* is a very rational being. We don't want them to flip-flop around unpredictably based on no evidence. Ideally, I would argue we want them to be even more rational than humans. I want my AI systems to be better at representing and manipulating probabilities than humans are. If we fast forward to today, the paper you wrote was a decade ago. How good are the AI systems that we're all used to playing around with at representing uncertainty? They've been getting better, but they're not very good. If you interact with a large language model and it asserts something, you can ask it, "How confident are you?" and it might say something back, but it's really doing next-token prediction. It doesn't have an explicit representation of how confident it is. It's not calculating Bayes' rule—at least, not explicitly. It may be because you've trained it on trillions of tokens from the web, it's mimicked a lot of other reasoning traces. It's sort of faking it. You can tell it's faking it, because if you push back and say something silly like, "No, I think you're wrong," it might respond, "Oh, sorry, yes, I was wrong." It's not really coherent. It's not going to stand its ground. Our systems are getting better at factual grounding, but we haven't really nailed the idea of how one of these models should represent probabilities over its beliefs. Why do they struggle with that so much? They struggle because the paradigm for training them hasn't prioritized that. We train them to be just really good at modeling the data. If the data involves a lot of human reasoning by many different humans with many different beliefs, then what you get is a soup—a mishmash of everything. If we want to build self-driving cars or robots, they need to reason about the real world. They need to have an understanding of cause and effect, their own uncertainty, and they need to use that to act in the real world in a safe way. I'm thinking about hallucinations here, because that's part of this as well, right? Sometimes there's a verifiable fact that it's getting wrong. That all plays into this. Hallucinations are a symptom. Sometimes we want our models to hallucinate; there's a tension between not hallucinating at all and being creative. If I want a model to write me a short story about the time Albert Einstein went to the moon in a rocket, that's clearly a hallucination, but it's an act of creative writing. It should be able to infer that's what you want. If I ask a factual question, it should try to be grounded. Even information on the web is often contradictory, so you want to hedge your bets. You would like a system that's able to tell you, for any statement, some estimate of its belief or probability. I think that's what we want. We don't have it yet. How might you build it if you wanted large language models to have uncertainty? There are some quite good ideas. Thinking about "semantic entropy," which one of your students came up with—how might that work? You can take the internals of a model and try to infer from that the degree of belief. Before it answers, before it produces a token, in a large language model, you have a probability distribution over all possible next tokens. The entropy of that distribution tells you something about the uncertainty. A low-entropy distribution is very spiky, very certain. A high-entropy distribution is very spread out, very uncertain. There are ways of teasing from the internals how certain it might be. I guess I'm thinking here about the Eiffel Tower in Paris. Those two things would be an example of something that's quite spiky. If you ask, "Where is the Eiffel Tower?" it should have a high probability over Paris. Although I believe there's one in New York and one in Vegas as well. Depending on the context, you might have little bits of entropy on these other options, but Paris would have a big spike. Is that the idea, that in the dataset, Paris and the Eiffel Tower appear near each other a lot? You have a big signal there, whereas the Eiffel Tower and Marrakesh might have very low probability. The problem with that is it's faking it; you're relying on the data. It's like trying to build a calculator just by showing it examples of addition and multiplication. If you never show it a particular number, it might not generalize. You don't want to fake a calculator; you want one that actually calculates. That's what we want from our AI systems. So why hasn't this been done yet? It's genuinely hard because it's computationally intensive. I would argue we had all the ingredients of AI maybe 15 or 20 years ago. We know how to build rational systems; we just thought that representing probability distributions over every possible thing was computationally intractable. It would take giant supercomputers that we didn't have and would be incredibly slow. So people abandoned that for just training from data. I think we can revisit this idea. I have ideas for how to do this, and I'm exploring them now, but it's not well-formed yet. Moving away from the transformer-type system, there are other examples of cutting-edge AI that handle uncertainty in this Bayesian way. I'm thinking about weather forecasting. Yes, that's really important. We've developed a whole series of state-of-the-art weather forecasting models at Google DeepMind. If you look at the GenCast model, it can predict weather over 15 days, and it can do it in eight minutes rather than on a giant supercomputer for hours. It uses neural networks, but a key ingredient is that it uses a diffusion model—which is already manipulating probability distributions over time—and then it generates an ensemble of forecasts. If you're tracking a tropical storm, like Hurricane Melissa, you have the data so far, and you want to forecast the track into the future because your decisions—like evacuating a city or calling in emergency services—depend on that. It represents that with an ensemble of forecasts, a whole probability distribution over the possible tracks. You rerun the model over and over, and as you get more data, you update that ensemble. That is essentially applying these basic ideas from Bayesian updating. The original weather forecasting models didn't have this; that was added on. And every time we add on these features, it makes the model better because you're essentially saying there is an inherent uncertainty in the way that weather works. It's a combination of inherent randomness—the classic butterfly effect in weather—and uncertainty because we have limited numbers of sensors. It represents its beliefs about the weather, and those beliefs get updated as you take in sensor measurements. I think there's something quite delightfully counterintuitive about that: you add uncertainty into the system, and it makes the predictions more accurate. It's not that you're making the system noisy in an arbitrary way. You're being honest about the fact that your sensors are inaccurate, sometimes you get faulty data, and sometimes your model assumptions are wrong. All those different forms of uncertainty have to be represented somehow. Approximately—we can't do it all exactly—so that we can get better-calibrated forecasts. The other example that really manages to get this uncertainty idea is AlphaFold, where the protein prediction is color-coded by how sure the model is that it’s the correct folding. You're fundamentally trying to solve an uncertain problem, going from a sequence to the folded structure. Physics itself means that some parts of the protein are going to wiggle around more, so you don't know exactly where they are. You also have uncertainty because you've used a model to predict that, not experimental data. You need to represent that uncertainty in the cloud of forecasts. There is a danger here that you could go too far. You could end up with a model that is so honest about its uncertainty that it never really gives you an answer, just hedging all the time. That would be funny. But the balance is: if there's a repeatable event in the real world, you want to be calibrated. If I say the chance of rain is 70%, then for all those days, if I sum up over all of them, it should actually rain on 70% of those days. That's calibrated probability. Now, if you ask me about something that's not a repeatable event, like the first day a human reaches Mars—that's a date. It's either going to happen or not. You can have beliefs over it, and it will resolve itself when it happens. Bayes' rule has a way of handling that, too. How do you communicate that uncertainty in a way that adds value, rather than having a human take cognitive shortcuts when the machine says it's really confident? I think people have different reactions. Some are skeptical and won't believe anything; others are going to be reliant. Imagine a future in the medical domain where you have doctors aided by AI looking at patients and test results. It's a great example of why we need uncertainty. If the AI system says something, you want it to convey its uncertainty, because that determines your treatment plan. These could be life and death decisions. It's essential that we don't become reliant on overconfident systems. We need them to be honest and bring that uncertainty in a visible form to the human user. My colleague at Cambridge, David Spiegelhalter, has done amazing work on how to convey uncertainties and probabilities in elegant ways to the general public. I believe it's important to have AI systems that are complementary and additive to humans. It feels like this is critical to get right. It's an important part of designing our collective future with AI. I don't want to take away from the fact that AI systems have been incredibly useful already. They're pretty good at some things. We can do better, and there are open problems along the way. I'm sitting here agreeing with you; you're also a Bayesian thinker. But not everybody agrees. There are people who say, "Look, you just put in more data and you don't need to worry about uncertainty because the model will know everything." That is a view a lot of people have, and they're not completely wrong. The models are pretty good. The problem is when you stretch them into the long tail of unusual things, you uncover gaps. If you're just interacting with a chatbot, the decisions aren't so consequential, but for self-driving cars or medical systems, we care about getting those probabilities right. When it comes to building AGI, there are two camps: one says you just need scale—more data, more compute—and the other says we need a new architecture to do things better. It sounds like you're in the second camp. I think we've made a lot of progress in the first. Our systems are useful and used by billions of people. But that doesn't mean we've run out of interesting things to discover. I'll give you examples of important research areas. One is continual learning. We train a giant model, release it, then a few months later train another. If you compare that to how humans and animals learn, we learn continuously. We're constantly getting a stream of data and adapting our connections. Our systems don't do that well; they suffer from catastrophic forgetting. That links back to Bayesian thinking. Because we learn by incorporating evidence with existing knowledge, that update system doesn't suffer from catastrophic forgetting. Many of our attempts at continual learning in large neural networks are approximations of that Bayesian updating. Another area is energy efficiency. The power consumption of a human brain is about 20 watts—it's a light bulb. A rubbish light bulb! A very energy-efficient one, let's say. If you compare that to training a large language model in a data center, it's orders of magnitude off. We can afford to do more research in energy-efficient learning. I also think there may be new architectures to discover. The workhorses are transformers and diffusion models; they're great, but there may be other evolutions, like novel hardware architectures or very sparse neural networks. Also, our learning systems are incredibly data-inefficient compared to human learning. Bayesian ideas can help there. It sounds like you've discovered a magic trick. This magic trick—embedding humility and uncertainty—offers the opportunity for continual learning and makes learning more data-efficient. It sounds too good to be true. It is! Obviously, we've known this for a long time. The magic trick comes with a big curse: to do all of this is computationally very slow. Textbooks explain how to do these things, but they say we can't do them exactly because they're computationally hard—NP-complete or NP-hard problems. We need to approximate them. You could argue that our modern AI systems are approximations to that. Can we have our cake and eat it too? They were saying that neural networks were too slow in the 80s, and you're not going to make that mistake twice. I think we can revisit these ideas with the compute power we have now. The parallel connection machine computer I used in my undergraduate years is slower than the Pixel phone I have in my pocket. Computation is getting better and faster, and we have decades of ideas on how to approximate these methods efficiently. We have the tools; we just need to put them together. Everything we've discussed feels like very human-like qualities. Did you imagine, when you started all these years ago, that this would be the thing our systems are lacking, rather than just computational power? I certainly didn't imagine we would be where we are now, because I think any AI researcher would say they're stunned by the rate of progress. But I also think these are fundamental qualities of intelligent systems. We need a concept of intelligence that transcends humans, because we have flaws in our reasoning. We miscalculate or misestimate probabilities. If we build human-centric AI systems, we work backwards from the biggest problems humanity faces, and for those, I would rather have a system that knows when it doesn't know than one that is arrogant and overconfident. What an amazing point to end on. Zoubin, thank you so much. That was brilliant. Thank you, Hannah. For years, we tried to build AI that was focused on accuracy, building systems that crunch through enormous amounts of data to land an answer. These things are astonishingly capable, of course, but they're also quite brittle in some ways. When they fail, they fail with total confidence. But by teaching AI to embrace uncertainty, it gives it something more human: humility and honesty, and the wisdom to doubt. And this isn't a vulnerability; it doesn't make AI weaker. It grounds it in reality, making it a collaborator we can actually rely on.

Wētā FX on the Future of AI-Powered Storytelling: S3 E5

AMD(680.0K)·25:04·1.5K views·2026-08-26
Mark PapermasterCTO and EVP, AMD Kimball ThurstonCTO, Wētā FX
TL;DRWētā FX CTO Kimball Thurston and AMD CTO Mark Papermaster explore how AI, heterogeneous computing, and simulation are transforming visual effects for major blockbusters like *Avatar 3* and *Stranger Things*.
Massive Scene Complexity: Wētā FX produces 20 to 25 shows...

TL;DR

Wētā FX CTO Kimball Thurston and AMD CTO Mark Papermaster explore how AI, heterogeneous computing, and simulation are transforming visual effects for major blockbusters like *Avatar 3* and *Stranger Things*.

Production Scale and Compute Architecture

  • Massive Scene Complexity: Wētā FX produces 20 to 25 shows annually—including *Superman*, *Avatar 3: The Way of Water and Fire*, and *Stranger Things*—handling scenes with hundreds of millions of elements, such as thousands of zombies in *The Last of Us* or entire alien planets in *Avatar*.
  • Render Farm Demands: The studio utilizes a massive compute farm running 500,000 threads 24/7.
  • CPU vs. GPU Workloads: Offline rendering workloads and massive scene files (which do not fit on a single graphics card) still rely heavily on CPUs driven by RAM requirements, while interactive tasks like hair braiding require real-time local GPU processing.
  • Unified Memory Adoption: Wētā FX is leveraging AMD Strix Halo boxes featuring unified memory, allowing CPUs, GPUs, and NPUs to access the same memory block locally without copying or synchronization overhead.

AI Integration and Creative Workflows

  • Asset Discovery: Wētā FX has built proprietary AI search engines that help artists quickly find and pull generic or movie-specific assets from massive libraries into scenes.
  • Procedural Muscle Simulation: Wētā FX research teams create custom models to simulate the muscles beneath a human face, allowing artists to apply subtle changes (like a smirk) via a brush tool that auto-simulates realistic facial behavior without manually adjusting thousands of curves.
  • Agentic Workflows: Artists are beginning to deploy AI agents to run asynchronous background tasks overnight—such as processing assets or checking if data is ready for publish—much like traditional 18-hour render jobs.
  • Economic Optimization: The industry is actively working through tokenomics and cost challenges by optimizing simulation engines, memory usage, and scene scopes to only render necessary environments.

Open Source and Intellectual Property

  • Community Collaboration: Wētā FX relies heavily on open-source tools and participates in the Academy Software Foundation, hosting growing open-source projects.
  • Provenance Tracking: Wētā FX recently open-sourced a model training refinement project called the Rongotai Model Train Club (a play on words for model training).
  • IP Transparency: The open-source project tracks the provenance of models used in simulation setups, recording what models were used to provide studio assurance that IP-unfriendly practices are avoided.

The Future of Immersive Storytelling

  • World of Models vs. World Models: Kimball Thurston dismisses singular "world models" as overzealous due to human diversity, predicting instead a "world of models"—a collection of distinct, captured environment and character models similar to current asset libraries.
  • Dynamic Digital Experiences: Pointing to Ben Grossman’s work on the *Wizard of Oz* experience at the Sphere in Las Vegas, Thurston highlights a shift toward software-driven, dynamically updated digital experiences that receive regular bug fixes and extensions over time.
  • Expanded Creative Palettes: Future workflows will bridge cinema experiences with personalized side adventures and branching storylines using local AI and cloud infrastructure.

Notable Quotes

"We're on the precipice of a sort of sea change across the entire world... not just in the movie making space. Where we can automate tasks that people might have had to do manually, but there's also equally a new creative palette that will unlock, by being able to better capture and represent the world that is around us." — Kimball Thurston
"It's always about story first. And it's the small details that if you get those small details right, it doesn't distract from the story... when you're doing large scale storytelling on a 150-foot screen, IMAX screen, or whatever it might be, those small details are all of a sudden very big." — Kimball Thurston
"There's a lot of talk about world models... I think this is overzealous... where when you look at the world, we are a diverse collection of a whole bunch of people, right? And so it's gonna be more like a world of models, not a world model." — Kimball Thurston
Welcome to Advanced Insights, where we provide just what the show name suggests: advanced insights into some of the most exciting trends and topics in technology. Kimball leads Wētā FX's future technology strategy for VFX production, overseeing the user-facing tools, research, and render software that powers the studio's evolving pipeline. He brings 30 years of VFX experience and has played a leadership role across Wētā's proprietary rendering technologies, engineering, and architecture teams. Kimball, welcome to the show. You pack up the truck, and you drive across country, and all of a sudden, 30 years later, you find yourself still doing it. You know, that's like the proverbial story. Like, "I packed my car, I went to Hollywood" story. Yeah, that's right. It is absolutely that. Well, you think about how what you do touches so many people. These movies just go global, and you are impacting the experience that so many people have in storytelling. What is your passion right now? What is it that's got you excited? Well, I mean, I think we're on the precipice of a sort of a sea change across the entire world, right? It's not just in the movie-making space. You mean with AI or? With AI, and I think there's multiple layers to that, right? There are some things where we can automate tasks that people might have had to do manually, but there's also equally a new creative palette that it will unlock by being able to better capture and represent the world that is around us, and use that as a storytelling vehicle. Where we can capture the 3D environment—the world, if you will. Yeah, that's an amazing vision—that we're gonna be moving from 2D to surrounding, 3D storytelling. And I think there are a lot of people who are very afraid of change. It's very natural to feel these feelings. But when you look historically, there have been changes. The switch from film to digital was gonna completely destroy the entire industry. Well, actually, job growth grew. And so I think that you'll see that with AI, as people start to understand how to use that as a tool to tell different kinds of stories. How do we add a richness to the story, the backstories, or the little side adventure you might have that's your own personalized side adventure? These kinds of things will probably create jobs in the end. Some jobs will change, but other things will grow. What does it take? What kind of compute and scale do you need as you create these amazing digital effects? Maybe for our audience, it might be good to just preface what are some of the movies that you've been behind in the past year?
