We probably end up doing 20 or 25 shows in a year. A massive reach of the technology output of your Wētā FX team. So, back to the question: what does it take to compute and create those types of digital effects? Is there a huge compute farm you need? Is it all in the cloud? Is it in your local shop? Yes, I think it's probably all of those things. When we're creating these massive worlds, you need a large amount of compute, and there's a huge amount of complexity to those worlds. They're very complex. Hundreds and hundreds of millions of different elements are in any one scene. Some of them are instances, like a tree—there are copies of a tree or something like this. Even for shows like The Last of Us, you have thousands of zombies attacking a wall. You've got either huge numbers of creatures, or you look at Avatar, where we're creating an entire planet's worth of environment. These things don't fit on a graphics card. In fact, we've built AI search engines to help people find these assets faster and get them pulled into a scene faster, where you can say, "Hey, I need something that looks like this," and it shows you a few different options. Artists are creating stuff based on a set design, and then you want things to be consistent over time. In AMD, just in our chip design, we're reimagining this. Are you starting to deploy AI in agentic workflows, and what impact is that having on your world? Yes. The longer answer is that we're trying to rethink how that production flow happens. From simple things like when we're bringing an asset in—is that ready for publish? Is the next person down the line gonna receive data that's ready for what they need to do? Integrating all of these kinds of operations into everyone's daily lives is the goal. Like, "Here's what I want the scene to be, and here's what I want you to create." The agent could be pulling out some of these libraries, and again, the creativity is still in the artist. But is there yet another wave of speed up coming? Probably. We are constantly looking for ways of doing that. We create a lot of our own models. We have a research team creating models, and one of the things we've done is simulate the muscles behind a person's face. We can't dissect them, so we have to estimate it. But then that is able to be a brush, so when someone says, "Hey, we want them to smirk a little bit," they can do that, and it's simulating the muscles such that you get all the right behavior across the rest of the face. They don't have to manually adjust five different curves; they're just indicating a little motion. That's a precursor of where we can get to—a future where people can stop having to micromanage thousands of curves. It's very significant to me what you said, because I think back to gaming. You used to see characters wearing a hat, why? Because we didn't have the physics and the polygon compute capability to show how the hair would move as someone was running or the wind blew through it. Well, now, for a long time in games, you see that hair flowing because we have the physics engines. It models that, and it looks so much more lifelike. Ray tracing of light, light bouncing around—same thing. What you're describing is that on steroids with digital effects. You're literally modeling the muscle reactions under the skin, and that's simulated with physics so that you get the right responses. Yeah, it's always about story first. It's the small details: if you get those right, it doesn't distract from the story. When someone sees something that's not quite right, they start looking for other things that are not quite right. When you're doing large-scale storytelling on a 150-foot IMAX screen, those small details are suddenly very big. You have to have everything right, from the large scale down to the tiniest of details. You're running agentic flows, but in parallel, each of those is rendering, so you're running across a heterogeneous mix. You're needing to run parallel, GPU-based computation. You're probably using CPUs for a number of these orchestration tasks and even some of the rendering. I know a lot of rendering is actually still staying in the CPU space. Yeah, that's mostly driven by RAM. The size of the scenes just doesn't fit on graphics cards. So we still do a lot on the CPU. What you pointed out there is exactly one of the things that I think is more the future: how do you have that heterogeneous mix of different ways of producing content? That's quite a demand on your computation, and you guys are experts at how to run that on the right engine at the right time. But you're also, as you said earlier, deploying AI. It's speeding up your process, and you're creating these agentic workflows. How does tokenomics play into how you are progressing here? The movies of the complexity you described—I would think tokenomics has got to be a huge issue. The bill could be astronomical. Absolutely. That's one of the things the industry is struggling with a little bit, trying to understand where and how to use these new tools. Artists are having to turn around and optimize their scene to fit within that budget. That's a more likely path where we can use these tools to not necessarily create the entire world, but just create the portion of the world that the story is happening in. One of the things you mentioned is partnership, and I'm so thankful for the way Wētā FX and AMD have been partners. We can run up to a 70 to 200 billion parameter model locally on our Strix Halo chips, and that'll keep going up generationally. It seems to me you guys embrace being the cutting edge and really pushing boundaries. Yeah, well, we're already doing that. Let those run while I'm focusing on something here. Most of our team is already used to that real-time feedback for interactive tasks while they have something running asynchronously in the background. When someone wants to make a modeling change—like adjusting the hair you spoke about earlier and how it's braided—they can't wait 400 milliseconds for that to go up to the cloud and come back. That has to be running on the GPU. If that involves a lookup for what style of braids are in vogue today, it needs to be local. Is that a challenge you'd give us at AMD and the rest of the industry? Is lowering latency and improving the capability of what can be run locally versus the cloud—giving you better tokenomics—a bottleneck you see to stay on the pace of adopting the cutting edge? Yeah, absolutely. Obviously, speaking of current events, we have to solve the memory issue that's hitting the world right now. But that's where platforms like the Strix Halo boxes you have are very interesting to us because they have that unified memory. We can have things on the CPU and the GPU addressing the same memory without having to copy it back and forth or keep them in sync, because they're just the same block of memory. These are really exciting to us. You are definitely an early adopter. I will say most users don't take advantage of unified memory, and most off-the-shelf software isn't thinking about how you can keep data resonant and operate on it from a CPU, a GPU, and an NPU. Now with Strix Halo, we have a huge local memory pool that can be attacked by all three of those accelerator engines. We're just in the early days here, and we're really excited about the space.
